\displaystyle\text{Re}\left(\frac{\Delta\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a\Delta)}\right)\geq\frac{y\tanh(y)\text{sec}^{2}(y)}{1+\tan^{2}(y)}\geq 0.

Also, asymptotically
\gamma_{a}
approaches the
-\frac{\pi}{4}
radians line so we have
\text{Re}\left(\frac{\Delta\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a\Delta)}\right)=\Theta(\sqrt{a})
.
∎

See
5.2.2

Proof.

The condition pdf
f
of
\int_{t}^{t+2\Delta}V(s)ds
can then be computed via inverse Fourier transform as:

\displaystyle f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{Re}[e^{-iax}\Phi(a)da].

(B.14)

If we only include the
a
-dependent quantities, we get the following:

\displaystyle\Phi(a)=C_{3}^{\prime}\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_a)}e^{-C_{1}\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a)}}I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_a)}\right),

(B.15)

where
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{3}^{\prime}
are

\displaystyle C_{1}=\frac{V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle C_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{V(t)V(t+2\Delta)}}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle C_{3}=\frac{e^{C_{1}\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}}\gamma_{a}\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\kappa\sinh(\gamma_a\Delta)}

\displaystyle C_{3}^{\prime}=\frac{C_{3}}{I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}.

We will want to keep track of terms that can grow with
V(t)
and ignore dependencies on non-asymptotic, model parameters like
\kappa,\Delta,\sigma
. Also, we will make use of some simple and useful properties of
\gamma_{a}
mentioned previously.

The first step is to determine a truncated approximation for the improper integral. We have that

\displaystyle\lvert\int_{m}^{\infty}\text{Re}[e^{-iax}\Phi(a)da]\rvert\leq\int_{m}^{\infty}\lvert\Phi(a)\rvert da.

(B.16)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{(x/2)^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}<I_{\nu}(x)<\frac{(x/2)^{\nu}e^{x}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)},

so by Lemma
B.3
,

\displaystyle\lvert\frac{I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}\leq\frac{I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\lvert\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\rvert\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(C_{2}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}\leq\left(\frac{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\Delta\kappa}\right)^{\xi}e^{\sqrt{2}C_{2}/\Delta}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\lvert\int_{m}^{\infty}\text{Re}[e^{-iax}\Phi(a)da]\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\left(\frac{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\Delta\kappa}\right)^{\xi}e^{\sqrt{2}C_{2}/\Delta}\int_{m}^{\infty}e^{-C_{1}\text{Re}(\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a)})}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{4(V(t)+V(t+2\Delta))}\int_{m}^{\infty}e^{-[V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)]\sqrt{a}}da\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{4(V(t)+V(t+2\Delta))}\int_{\sqrt{m}}^{\infty}xe^{-[V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)]x}dx\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{e^{4(V(t)+V(t+2\Delta))}}{V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)}\sqrt{m}e^{-[V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)]\sqrt{m}}\right),

so to ensure a truncation error
\epsilon_{\text{imp}}
, we can take
m=\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}(1/\epsilon_{\text{imp}}))
.

We can thus restrict to approximating just
\int_{0}^{m}\text{Re}[e^{-iax}\Phi(a)da]
.

For our setting
z=\gamma_{a}
whose real part upper bounds the imaginary in magnitude, and so
z\in\mathcal{R}
from Lemma
F
. Also
\lvert z\rvert
is
\Theta(\sqrt{a})=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{m})
asymptotically from Lemma
B.3
.
Thus the conditions of Lemma
F
are satisfied, and we can approximate both
\frac{z}{\sinh(z)}
and
\frac{z}{\tanh(z)}
to
\epsilon
additive error using
\mathcal{O}(\log(\sqrt{m} /\epsilon))
degree polynomials. Also
m
is
\mathcal{O}\left(\log^{2}(1/\epsilon_{\text{imp}})\right)
due to the truncation, giving a degree of
\mathcal{O}(\log(\log(1/\epsilon_{\text{imp}})/\epsilon))
.

For the modified Bessel function we have the globally-convergent power series

\displaystyle I_{\alpha}(z)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(z/2)^{2m+\alpha}}{m!\Gamma(m+\alpha+1)},z\in\mathbb{C},

(B.17)

and so if
\lvert z\rvert\leq M
, then the truncation index scales as
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(M/\epsilon\right)\right)
. So we take
M=\mathcal{O}\left(C_{2}\right)
, giving a degree scaling of
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(C_2C_3'/\epsilon)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left([V(t)+V(t+2\Delta)]\log(V(t) + V(t+2\Delta)/\epsilon)\right)
. This is also roughly the same for the exponential term
e^{-C_{1}\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a)}}
, except for an additional factor of
\sqrt{m}=\log(1/\epsilon_{\text{imp}})
, which goes under a log anyways. Hence the same degree scaling holds.

Thus we have the following expression for the error from polynomial approximation via triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\lvert(\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}\pm\epsilon)\left(e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}\pm\epsilon}\pm\epsilon\right)\left(I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)\pm\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}}I_{\alpha}\left(C_{3}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\right)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\lvert\left(e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}\pm\epsilon}\pm\epsilon\right)\left(I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)\pm\epsilon\right)\rvert

\displaystyle+\lvert\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}\epsilon\left(I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)\pm\epsilon\right)\rvert

\displaystyle+\lvert\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}}\left(I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)\pm\epsilon\right)\rvert\lvert 1-e^{\pm\epsilon}\rvert

\displaystyle+\lvert(\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))})\left(e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}}\right)\epsilon\rvert

\displaystyle+\lvert\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}\left(e^{-C_{1}\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))}}\right)\left(I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)-I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\right)\right)\rvert.

(B.18)

Recall
\lvert\frac{z(a)}{\sinh(z(a))}\rvert\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\Delta}
. Note that
I_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(I_{\alpha-1}(z)+I_{\alpha+1}(z)\right)
and so

\displaystyle\lvert I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\pm\epsilon\right)-I_{\alpha}\left(C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\right)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\lvert z-C_{2}\frac{z(a)}{\sinh{z(a)}}\rvert\leq\epsilon}\lvert I^{\prime}_{\alpha}(z)\rvert\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq I_{\alpha+1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{2}}{\Delta}+1\right)\epsilon

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),

and recall
e^{-C_{1}\text{Re}(\frac{z(a)}{\tanh(z(a))})}<1
. So Equation (
B.3
) is
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
.

Thus
\Phi(a)
can be
\epsilon
-uniformly approximated by a
{\mathcal{O}}\left((\max_{t}V(t))^{2}\log^{2}(\max_{t}V(t)/\epsilon)\right)
degree polynomial on a compact domain. The squaring comes from approximating
e^{C_{1}\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_a)}}
by a composition of polynomials.

We then need to integrate the terms in the series, which will fit to the form of the lower incomplete Gamma function. Recall the lower incomplete Gamma function:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{m}(s+1):=\int_{0}^{m}t^{s}e^{-t}dt,

(B.19)

which can be expressed as the the following power series valid for
m\in\mathbb{C}
and
s
not a non-positive integer:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{m}(s+1)=m^{s+1}\sum_{k=0}\frac{(-m)^{k}}{k!(s+k)},

(B.20)

which has a truncation index of
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\lvert m \rvert/\epsilon)\right)
for
\epsilon
additive error. We will also need to account for the various scaling factors
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}^{\prime}
. However, one will note that the degree asymptotics is still no more than anything we have previously estimated.

∎

Appendix C
Additional Proofs for Section
6.1

C.1
Proof of Theorem
6.1

See
6.1

Proof.

There are two kinds of primitive distributions one-dimensional
\chi^{2}
with
\eta-1
degrees of freedom
p_{\chi}
and standard Gaussian
p_{\mathcal{N}}
.

We will be using fixed-point arithmetic. The arithmetic will be done on a grid over
[-b,b]^{2T}
with
N
grid points per dimension.

From Corollary
5.2.1
, we can load a quantum state that
\epsilon
approximates the measurement distribution of
5.2
in the computational basis with
N=\Omega\left(b/\epsilon_{\textup{distr}}\right)
grid points, where
p
is standard Gaussian over
[-b,b]
using

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(b\log(N)\log(b/\epsilon_{\text{distr}})\right)

one- and two-qubit gates. Corollary
5.2.1
provides an analogous result for the central
\chi^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left((b+r)\log(N)\log(b/\epsilon_{\text{distr}})\right),

with
N=\Omega\left(br/\epsilon_{\textup{distr}}\right)
.

Note we will have
2T
primitive distributions in total, and hence by Equation (
4.11
), we take

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\text{distr}}\mapsto\frac{\epsilon_{\text{distr}}}{2T\max_{\vec{x}\in[-b,b]^{T}\times[0,b]^{T}}\lvert f\circ g(\vec{x})\rvert}.

By Lemma
C.2
and Lipschitzness of
f
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\vec{x}\in[-b,b]^{T}\times[0,b]^{T}}\lvert f\circ g(\vec{x})\rvert=\mathcal{O}\left(BT^{2}b^{2}\right),

so we scale all
\epsilon_{\text{distr}}
by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\text{distr}}\mapsto\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{\text{distr}}}{T\cdot BT^{2}b^{2}}\right).

Thus the total gate count, after scaling the error, for the
2T
Gaussians and
\chi^{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(T(b+r)\log(N)\log(BTb/\epsilon_{\text{distr}})\right),

with
N=\Omega\left(BT^{3}b^{3}r/\epsilon_{\text{distr}}\right)
.

We then need to coherently compute the recursion

\displaystyle|x\rangle|z\rangle|y\rangle|0\rangle\mapsto|x\rangle|z\rangle|y\rangle|c(y+(z+\sqrt{\beta x})^{2})\rangle,

c
and
\beta
are fixed.

Over
T
steps this requires
2T
additions,
3T
multiplications/scalings and
T
square-roots. The precision to which we have each
z
and
y
is at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(N)\right)
. We can for simplicity suppose that the initial point
x_{0}
is also to this precision.

Due to the recursive nature of the path construction for the CIR process, there is a potential for the error to grow. Let the iteration at the
t
-th step be

\displaystyle x_{t}=c(y_{t}+(z_{t}+\sqrt{\beta x_{t-1}})^{2}),

(C.1)

where all operations are done to
n
bits of fixed-point arithmetic. The only approximate operation is the inverse square-root computed via Newton’s method (See Section
G.1
), which we denote by
\epsilon_{\text{NM}}
. Consider the following example

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{1}=c(y_{1}+(z_{1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{0}}\pm\epsilon_{\text{NM}})^{2})=x_{1}+2c(z_{1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{0}})\epsilon_{\text{NM}}+c\epsilon_{\text{NM}}^{2}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{2}=c(y_{2}+(z_{2}+\sqrt{\beta\widetilde{x}_{1}}\pm\epsilon_{\text{NM}})^{2})

\displaystyle\leq c(y_{2}+(z_{2}+\sqrt{\beta x_{1}}+\sqrt{2c(z_{1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{0}})\epsilon_{\text{NM}}}+\sqrt{c}\epsilon_{\text{NM}}\pm\epsilon_{\text{NM}})^{2})

\displaystyle=x_{2}+(2c(z_{2}+\sqrt{\beta x_{1}})\sqrt{2c(z_{1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{0}})\epsilon_{\text{NM}}}+2c(\sqrt{c}+1)(z_{2}+\sqrt{\beta x_{1}})\epsilon_{\text{NM}}+o(\epsilon_{\text{NM}}).

In the next step the term
(2c(z_{2}+\sqrt{\beta x_{1}})\sqrt{2c(z_{1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{0}})\epsilon_{\text{NM}}}
appears in the upper bound, implying a lose of precision. The
\epsilon_{\text{NM}}
will need to be chosen such that the value under the square-root is
<1
, hence it can potentially grow to be significant (i.e. towards a constant). Here, we use a loose upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(b^{T}\epsilon_{\text{NM}}^{2^{-T}})
(
b
from Lemma
C.2
) after
t
steps. Thus, we need to consider
\epsilon_{\text{NM}}\mapsto(\epsilon_{\text{NM}}/b^{T})^{2^{T}}
.

Note that in the region of quadratic convergence of Newton’s method, there is a
\mathcal{O}(\log\log(1/\epsilon_{\text{NM}}))
iteration complexity. Hence the above combined with (
G.2
) gives a gate cost of
\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\log(N)^{2}\log(T\log(b/\epsilon_{\textup{NM}}))\right)
for
\mathcal{O}(T)
square-roots. We only need to ensure that the
\epsilon_{\text{NM}}
from Newton’s method is below
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
.

Next let us determine how many qubits are required for the output register, i.e. to hold the valeue
f\circ g_{t}
. Note that in (
C.1
), each time
z_{t}
is squared, unless it is the final time point, it gets square-rooted in the next step. Additionally, each
x_{t-1}
is square-rooted before squaring. Hence, ignoring the Newton error, the required number of bits to hold the output of the recursion does not grow with time. So without loss of generality, we can suppose
g_{t}
requires
\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(N))
bits to represent. The number of lower order bits is
\mathcal{O}(\log_{2}(N/b))
. Note that
f
can expand the output by at most a factor of
\mathcal{O}(BT)
. Hence
\mathcal{O}(\log(BTN))
bits also suffices for the output register.

The
\mathcal{O}(T)
other additions and multiplications/scalings cost together
\mathcal{O}(T\log(N)^{2})
gates. Along with the oracle for the payoff, we can perform the operation:

\displaystyle|\vec{z}\rangle|\vec{y}\rangle|0\rangle\rightarrow|\vec{z}\rangle|\vec{y}\rangle|f\circ g(\vec{z},\vec{y})\rangle

using

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(T^{2}\log^{2}(N)\log(T)+\mathcal{N}_{f}\right)

gates.

The rest now follows Lemma
B.2
, with
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}(BT^{2}b^{2})
. This gives
\mathcal{O}\left(\log^{3}(\Lambda N)\right)
additional gates to perform the rotation onto an amplitude.

∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
6.2

The recursion on
x_{t}
can be upper bounded over time using the following simple estimate.

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
X_{t}
follow a CIR process with
\gamma+\gamma^{2}<1
. Then any point
x_{t}
on a realized path satisfies

\displaystyle x_{t}\leq x_{0}+c(1+\gamma)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{t}y_{k}+z_{k}^{2}\right).

Proof.

Recall that
\gamma:=\sqrt{c\beta}<1
and let
v_{t}:=x_{t}/c
then

\displaystyle v_{t}=(y_{t}+(z_{t}+\gamma\sqrt{v_{t-1}})^{2}),

where from Jensen’s

\displaystyle v_{t}\leq y_{t}+(1+\gamma)z_{t}^{2}+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{t-1},

an inductive argument shows that

\displaystyle v_{t}

\displaystyle\leq(\gamma+\gamma^{2})^{t}v_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{t}(\gamma+\gamma^{2})^{t-k}(y_{k}+(1+\gamma)z_{k}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{x_{0}}{c}+\left(\sum_{k=0}^{t}y_{k}+(1+\gamma)z_{k}^{2}\right),

where we used Hölder’s inequality and
\gamma+\gamma^{2}<1
.
∎

We will truncate the densities for the the standard Gaussian and chi-square to
[-a,a]
and
[b_{L},b_{U}]
, respectively. The following result determines the scaling of the endpoints in terms of the truncation error
\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}
.

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Suppose the payoff
f
is upper bounded by a
B
-Lipschitz linear function in the path process
\widehat{V}
, then

\displaystyle b_{L}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{BT^{2}r}\right)

\displaystyle b_{U}=\mathcal{O}\left(r+\ln(BT/\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle a=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\ln(BT/\epsilon)}\right)

suffice for
\epsilon
truncation error.

Proof.

Let
S:=([b_{L},b_{U}]^{T}\times[-a,a]^{T})^{c}
, then via the previous lemma and i.i.d. of increments:

\displaystyle\int_{S}\lvert f\circ g(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})\rvert p(\vec{z})p(\vec{y})d\vec{y}d\vec{z}

\displaystyle\leq B\sum_{t}\int_{S}g_{t}p(\vec{z})p(\vec{y})d\vec{y}d\vec{z}

\displaystyle\leq c(1+\gamma)BT^{2}\left(x_{0}+\mathbb{E}[y]\int_{\lvert z\rvert\geq a}z^{2}p_{\mathcal{N}}(z)+\mathbb{E}[z^{2}]\int_{y\geq b}yp_{\chi}(y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq c(1+\gamma)BT^{2}r\left(x_{0}+\int_{\lvert z\rvert\geq a}z^{2}p_{\mathcal{N}}(z)+\int_{y\geq b}yp_{\chi}(y)\right).

For
a>1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\lvert z\rvert\geq a}z^{2}p_{\mathcal{N}}(z)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{z\geq a}z^{2}e^{-z^{2}/2}dz\leq 2ae^{-a^{2}/2},

Consider
a=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ln(1/\epsilon)})
suffices for an error that is
\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.

Recall that for the Gamma function
s\Gamma(s)=\Gamma(s+1)
, so

\displaystyle\int_{y\geq b}yp_{\chi}(y)

\displaystyle=2\int_{y\geq b}\frac{\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{r/2}e^{-y/2}}{2\Gamma(r/2)}

\displaystyle=2r\int_{y\geq b}\frac{\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{(r+2)/2-1}e^{-y/2}}{2\Gamma(\frac{r+2}{2})}

\displaystyle=2r\mathbb{P}[Y\geq b],

where
Y\sim\chi^{2}_{r+2}
. Note that by Chernoff bounding with
\lambda=\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[Y\geq b]\leq e^{-\lambda b}(1-2\lambda)^{-(r+2)/2}\leq e^{-\frac{b}{4}+\frac{(r+2)}{2}\ln(2)},

so
b_{U}=\mathcal{O}\left(r+\ln(1/\epsilon)\right)
suffices.

Recall that we assume at least
r\geq 2
and
b_{L}\ll 1
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\leq b_{L}}yp_{\chi_{r}}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\leq b_{L}}yp_{\chi_{2}}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\leq b_{L}}ye^{-y/2}dy

\displaystyle\leq b_{L}

so
b_{L}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{BT^{2}r}\right)
suffices.

∎

See
6.2

Proof.

By the piecewise linear assumption, we can upper bound the partial derivatives by the partial derivatives of

\displaystyle[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}Bx_{t}]p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})=[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}Bg_{t}(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})]p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y}).

We need to compute the various partial derivatives:
\partial_{y_{k}},\partial_{z_{k}}
. We will bound the derivatives by iterating on
x_{t}=c(y_{t-1}+(z_{t-1}+\sqrt{\beta x_{t-1}})^{2})=g_{t}(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})
. Recall that
\gamma<1
and from the recurrence for
x_{t}
:
\sqrt{\beta x_{t}}\geq\gamma\sqrt{y_{t-1}}
.

The chain-rule recursion (for
y
or
z
) is:

\displaystyle(x_{t})^{\prime}=c\beta\left(\frac{z_{t-1}}{\sqrt{\beta x_{t-1}}}+1\right)\cdot(x_{t-1})^{\prime}\leq\left(\frac{z_{t-1}}{\sqrt{y_{t-2}}}+1\right)\cdot(x_{t-1})^{\prime}

By our notation
x_{j+1}
is determined by
z_{j},y_{k}
and
x_{j}
. Let
T
be arbitrary and
T>j+1
. Hence,

\displaystyle|\partial_{x_{j+1}}g_{T-1}(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})|\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}p(z_{k})\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}p(y_{k})

\displaystyle=|\partial_{x_{j+1}}g_{T-1}(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})|\prod_{k=j+1}^{T-2}p(z_{k})\prod_{k=j}^{T-3}p(y_{k})\cdot[p(z_{j})\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}p(z_{k}),p(y_{k})]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=j+1}^{T-2}\left[\left(\frac{z_{k}}{\sqrt{y_{k-1}}}+1\right)p(z_{k})p(y_{k-1})\right]\cdot[p(z_{j})\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}p(z_{k}),p(y_{k})]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=j+1}^{T-2}\frac{z_{k}y_{k-1}^{r/2-3/2}+y_{k-1}^{r/2-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}e^{-\frac{y_{k-1}+z_{k}^{2}}{2}}\cdot[p(z_{j})\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}p(z_{k}),p(y_{k})].

(C.2)

Recall that
\Gamma(r/2)\geq(r/2e)^{r/2-1}
, for
r/2\geq 1
. Consider some
\ell,1\leq\ell\leq r/2
, then
y^{r/2-\ell}e^{-y/2}
is maximized at
y=r-2\ell
, so

\displaystyle\frac{y^{r/2-\ell}e^{-y/2}}{\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(r-2\ell)^{r/2-\ell}e^{-r/2+\ell}}{\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(r-2\ell)^{r/2-\ell}e^{\ell-1}}{2r^{r/2-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(1-2\ell/r)^{r/2-\ell}e^{\ell-1}.

Thus, for
r\geq 4
,

\displaystyle\frac{z_{k}y_{k-1}^{r/2-3/2}+y_{k-1}^{r/2-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}e^{-\frac{y_{k-1}+z_{k}^{2}}{2}}\leq\frac{(1-3/r)^{r/2-3/2}+(1-2/r)^{r/2-1}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle|\partial_{x_{j+1}}g_{T-1}(\vec{y},\vec{z},x_{0})|\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}p(z_{k})\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}p(y_{k})\leq 2^{-(T-j-3)}[p(z_{j})\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}p(z_{k}),p(y_{k})],

and so

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})\lVert\nabla g_{T-1}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{T-2}2^{-(T-j-3)}(\partial_{y_{j}}x_{j+1}+\partial_{z_{j}}x_{j+1})[p(z_{j})\prod_{k=0}^{j-1}p(z_{k}),p(y_{k})].

Also simply,

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{y}p_{\chi_{r}}(y)\rvert=\lvert\frac{[(r/2-1)y^{r/2-2}-(1/2)y^{r/2-1}]e^{-y/2}}{2^{r/2}\Gamma(r/2)}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}p_{\chi_{r-2}}(y_{k})+\frac{1}{2}p_{\chi_{r}}(y)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}p_{\mathcal{N}}(z)\leq\lvert z\rvert p_{\mathcal{N}}(z),

\displaystyle g_{T-1}\lVert\nabla(p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y}))\rVert_{\infty}\leq g_{T-1}\sum_{k=0}^{T-2}p(y_{\neq k},z_{\neq k})\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{\chi_{r-2}}(y_{k})+p_{\chi_{r}}(y_{k})\right)p_{\mathcal{N}}(z_{k})+\lvert z_{k}\rvert p_{\chi_{r}}(y_{k})p_{\mathcal{N}}(z_{k})\right]

Suppose we consider the left-endpoint rule in the region
(\vec{y},\vec{z})\in[R_{L},R]^{T}\times[-\frac{R}{2},\frac{R}{2}]^{T}
, where
R=2\max(a,b_{U})=\mathcal{O}\left(r+\log(BT/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})\right)
with
a,b_{U}
from Lemma
C.2
. We take the grid spacing to be
\frac{R}{N}
uniformly and denote the grid
\mathcal{M}
.

From Lemma
F
on the left-endpoint rule (for a single
x_{T}
), we get that the discretization error is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{T-2}\left(\sum_{(\vec{y},\vec{z})\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{R}(j,\vec{w}(\vec{y}),\vec{w}(\vec{z}))\prod_{k=1}^{2T}\Delta_{k}\right)\frac{2TR}{N},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(j,\vec{y},\vec{z})

\displaystyle:=2^{-(T-j-3)}\lVert\nabla_{(y_{j},z_{j})}x_{j+1}\rVert_{1}p(y_{\leq(j-1)},z_{\leq(j-1)})

\displaystyle+g_{T-1}\cdot p(y_{\neq j},z_{\neq j})\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{\chi_{r-2}}(y_{j})+p_{\chi_{r}}(y_{j})\right)p_{\mathcal{N}}(z_{j})+\lvert z_{j}\rvert p_{\chi_{r}}(y_{j})p_{\mathcal{N}}(z_{j})\right],

and
\vec{w}
maps
\vec{y}
and
\vec{z}
to the maximizer of
R(j,\cdot,\cdot)
in the cell with
(\vec{y},\vec{z})
as its lowest corner.

Now if for
N\geq N_{0}
(
N_{0}
independent of dimension) the volumes with respect to all of the truncated, one-dimensional pdfs present in the above expression are bounded by a constant, then we can upper each inner sum by the max of the term multiplied by each pdf times some constant. Without loss of generality, we can just look at sums of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{(\vec{y},\vec{z})\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}p(\vec{y},\vec{z})\prod_{k=1}^{2T}\Delta_{k}=\left(\sum_{\vec{z}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}}\frac{e^{-\lVert\vec{z}\rVert_{2}^{2}/2}}{(2\pi)^{(T-2)/2}}\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}\Delta_{k}\right)\left(\sum_{\vec{y}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\chi}}\frac{\left(\prod_{j=0}^{T-2}y_{j}\right)^{r/2-1}e^{-\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}/2}}{[\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}]^{T-2}}\prod_{k=0}^{T-2}\Delta_{k}\right)

(C.3)

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a modified version of the grid
\mathcal{M}
with each grid point
(\vec{y},\vec{z})
being replaced with
(\vec{w}(\vec{y}),\vec{w}(\vec{z}))
. Also
\mathcal{M}_{\chi}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{N}}^{\prime}
denote the grid split over the cartesian product of
\vec{y}
and
\vec{z}
.

By a similar analysis for GBM in Theorem
4.1
, the Gaussian sum can be made
\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
for
N=\mathcal{O}\left(\max(\sigma,1)T^{3/2}R\right)
.

Suppose we shift the grid
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{\chi}
such that each grid point
\vec{y}
is replaced with the minimizer of
p_{\chi}
over
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\vec{y},\Delta)
. On this new grid, the sum in the right factor is upper bounded by one. We then need to compute the error term from shifting the grid, which is done by looking at the relative change in the
\chi^{2}
pdf between the two grid points.

The relative change in the
\chi^{2}
pdf when going from
\vec{y}
to some
\vec{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{y}}=\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\vec{y},\Delta)
is bounded by (using
b_{L}
from Lemma
C.2
, so
\forall j,x_{j}=\Omega(\frac{\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}}{BT^{2}r})
)

\displaystyle\lvert\left(\prod_{j=0}^{T-2}\frac{y_{j}}{x_{j}}\right)^{r/2-1}e^{-(\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1})/2}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\left(\prod_{j=0}^{T-2}(1+\Delta/x_{j})\right)^{r/2-1}e^{-(\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}-\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1})/2}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{BT^{2}r\Delta}{\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}}\right)^{Tr}e^{T\Delta}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(e^{BT^{3}r^{2}\Delta/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}}).

Thus the above is
\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
with
\Delta=\mathcal{O}(\frac{\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}}{Br^{2}T^{3}})
, recall the number of bits is log in
\frac{R}{\Delta}
. Thus, the right sum in (
C.3
)involving the
\chi^{2}
pdf is bounded by a constant independent of
T
for
N=\Omega\left(\frac{BRr^{2}T^{3}}{\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}}\right)
.

Thus for
N=\Omega\left(BT^{3}R^{2}r^{2}/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}\right)
, where
R
is the maximum of the Gaussian and
\chi^{2}
truncation endpoints
a,b
, we have that Equation (
C.3
) is bounded by a constant independent of
T
. Then, if we combined the previous observation with Jensen’s inequality we get that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{T-2}\left(\sum_{(\vec{y},\vec{z})\in\mathcal{M}}\mathcal{R}(j,\vec{w}(\vec{y}),\vec{w}(\vec{z}))\prod_{k=1}^{2T}\Delta_{k}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\text{poly}(T,R)\right).

The asymptotics on the number of bits follows.
∎

Appendix D
Additional Proofs for Section
6.2

D.1
Heston Moment Explosions : Proof of
[
And07
, Proposition 3.1]

Proposition
\theTheorem

(Riccati equation)
.

Consider the Riccati ODE

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}y(x)=ay^{2}(x)+by(x)+c

with initial condition
y(0)=0
.
The solution to this ODE is given by

\displaystyle y=\frac{1}{2a}\left(\beta\tan(\frac{1}{2}\beta(x-C))-b\right),

where

\displaystyle\beta=\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}},\quad C=-\frac{2}{\beta}\arctan(\frac{b}{\beta}).

Proof.

Separating
x
and
y
and integrating on both sides, we have

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\int\frac{dy}{ay^{2}+by+c}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}}}\arctan(\frac{2 a y + b}{\sqrt{4 a c - b^2}})+C

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\beta}\arctan(\frac{2 a y + b}{\beta})+C.

Invert the equation above gives

\displaystyle y=\frac{1}{2a}\left(\beta\tan(\frac{1}{2}\beta(x-C))-b\right).

Applying the initial condition
y(0)=0
gives the value of
C
.
∎

Proposition
\theTheorem

(
[
AP07
, Proposition 3.1]
)
.

Consider a stochastic process
S_{t}
following the Heston SDEs given by
2.3
.
Consider
\omega>1
, then the
\omega
-th moment of
S_{t}
, i.e.
\mathbf{E}[S^{\omega}_{t}]
, is finite for all
t\in[0,T^{*})
and infinite for
t\geq T^{*}
, where

\displaystyle T^{*}=\begin{cases}\infty,&b^{2}-4ac\geq 0,b<0;\\
\frac{1}{\gamma}\log(\frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma}),&b^{2}-4ac\geq 0,b>0;\\
\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\pi-2\arctan(\frac{c}{\beta})\right),&b^{2}-4ac<0;\end{cases}

where
a=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2},b=\rho\sigma\omega-\kappa,c=\frac{\omega^{2}-\omega}{2}
,
\beta:=\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}}
, and
\gamma:=-i\beta
.

Proof.

Define
Z_{t}:=\log S_{t}
. Then, we have the pair of coupled SDEs:

\displaystyle dZ_{t}=(\mu-\frac{V_{t}}{2})dt+\sqrt{V(t)}dW_{Z},

\displaystyle dV_{t}=\kappa(\theta-V_{t}dt+\sigma\sqrt{V(t)}dW_{V}

\displaystyle dW_{Z}\cdot dW_{V}=\rho dt,

leading to

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{t}\\
V_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

\displaystyle d\mathbf{X}_{t}=\bm{\mu}(\mathbf{X}_{t})dt+\bm{\sigma}(\mathbf{X}_{t})d\mathbf{W}.

(D.1)

An application of the Kolmogorov backward equation to (
D.1
) shows that with
u\leq t
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{X}_{t})|(Z_{u},V_{u})=(z,v)]=\mathbb{E}[e^{\omega Z_{t}}|(Z_{u},V_{u})=(z,v)]=f(t,z,v)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[S^{\omega}_{t}]=f(0,Z(0),V(0)),

we have that
f(u,z,v)
satisfies the following PDE

\displaystyle\partial_{u}f+(\mu-\frac{v}{2})\partial_{z}f+\kappa(\theta-v)\partial_{v}f+\rho\sigma v\partial_{zv}f+\frac{\sigma^{2}v}{2}\partial_{v}^{2}f+\frac{v}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}f=0

(D.2)

subject to the final condition
f(t,z,v)=e^{\omega z}
.
The solution to the PDE is given in the form of

\displaystyle f(u,z,v)=e^{\omega z}e^{A(t-u)+vB(t-u)},

where
A(0)=B(0)=0
. We can then isolate
A
and
B
from Equation (
D.2
) to get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}A(\tau)=\kappa\theta B(\tau)+\mu\omega

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}B(\tau)=aB^{2}(\tau)+bB(\tau)+c,

where
a=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2},b=\rho\sigma\omega-\kappa,c=\frac{\omega^{2}-\omega}{2}
and
\tau:=t-u
.
Applying
Section

D.1
with
\beta=\sqrt{4ac-b^{2}}
gives the solution for
B(\tau)

\displaystyle B(\tau)=\frac{1}{2a}\left(\beta\tan(\frac{\beta}{2} (\tau- C))-b\right)

where

\displaystyle C=-\frac{2}{\beta}\arctan(\frac{b}{\beta}).

Now we analyze
B(\tau)
depending on the signs of
b^{2}-4ac
and
b
.

Case (3)
b^{2}-4ac<0
.

In this case,
\beta
is real, and
B(\tau)
is finite if and only if the
\tan
term is finite, or equivalently
\frac{\beta}{2}(\tau-C)<\frac{\pi}{2}
.
Substitute in
C
we have the range of
\tau
for
B(\tau)
being finite

\displaystyle\tau\in\left[0,\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\pi-2\arctan(\frac{b}{\beta})\right)\right).

Cases (1) & (2)
b^{2}-4ac\geq 0
.

In these cases,
\beta
is imaginary and
\gamma=-i\beta\geq 0
is real.
Rewriting
B(\tau)
in terms of
\gamma
we obtain

\displaystyle B(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2a}\left(i\gamma\tan(i\frac{\gamma}{2} (\tau- C))-b\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2a}\left(\gamma\tanh(\frac{\gamma}{2} (\tau- C))+b\right),

where we have used the identity
\tanh(x)=-i\tan(i x)
.
Similarly, we can rewrite
C
as

\displaystyle C=-\frac{2}{i\gamma}\arctan(\frac{b}{i \gamma})=\frac{2}{\gamma}\arctanh\left(\frac{b}{\gamma}\right)=\frac{1}{\gamma}\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{\gamma- b}),

where we have used the identity
i\arctan(-i x)=\arctanh(x)=\frac{1}{2}\log(\frac{1+x}{1-x})
.
Since
a,c>0
, we have
\absolutevalue{b}>\gamma
, and consequently

\displaystyle\frac{b+\gamma}{b-\gamma}=\frac{1+\gamma/b}{1-\gamma/b}>0.

Therefore
\log(\frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})
is real and we can write
C
as

\displaystyle C=\frac{1}{\gamma}\left(\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})+i\pi\right).

Substitute
C
into
B(\tau)
we obtain

\displaystyle B(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2a}\left(\gamma\tanh(\frac{\gamma}{2} \tau- \frac{1}{2} \log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma}) - i \frac{\pi}{2} )+b\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2a}\left(\gamma\coth(\frac{\gamma}{2}\tau-\frac{1}{2}\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma}))+b\right).

Observe that
\coth(x)
has a singularity at
0
and will be finite for all
x\neq 0
.
We will use this fact to determine the range of
\tau
for
B(\tau)
to be finite.
If
b<0
, then
\frac{b+\gamma}{b-\gamma}<1
, therefore
\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})<0
and hence
\frac{\gamma}{2}\tau-\frac{1}{2}\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})>0
for all
\tau\geq 0
, which means that the
\coth
term will be finite.
Consequently,
B(\tau)
will be finite for all
\tau\geq 0
.

On the other hand, when
b>0
, we have
\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})>0
, and hence the argument inside
\coth
will cross zero at
\tau=\frac{1}{\gamma}\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})
.
This means that
B(\tau)
will be finite for all
\tau\in\left[0,\frac{1}{\gamma}\log( \frac{b + \gamma}{b - \gamma})\right)
and becomes infinite for all larger
\tau
.
∎

Remark
\theTheorem
.

For the case of
b^{2}-4ac<0
,
[
AP07
, Proposition 3.1]
expressed the result as

\displaystyle T^{*}=\frac{2}{\beta}\left(\pi 1_{\{b<0\}}+\arctan(\frac{b}{\beta})\right).

We note that this is equivalent to our result due to the following identity

\displaystyle\arctan(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan(\frac{1}{x}),&x>0;\\
-\frac{\pi}{2}-\arctan(\frac{1}{x}),&x<0.\end{cases}

Remark
\theTheorem

(extension of
Section

6.2.3
to
\omega=1
)
.

In the case where
\omega=1
, we have
c=0
,
b^{2}-4ac=b^{2}\geq 0
and
\gamma=\absolutevalue{b}
.
Following the same argument as in the proof of
Section

6.2.3
for
b^{2}-4ac\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle C=\frac{2}{\gamma}\arctanh\left(\frac{b}{\gamma}\right)=\frac{2}{\gamma}\arctanh\left(\mathrm{sign}(b)\right)=\mathrm{sign}(b)\infty.

Therefore
B(\tau)\equiv 0
for all
\tau\geq 0
, and hence the first moment of
S(t)
always exists.

D.2
Proof of Theorem
6.4

See
6.4

Proof.

The arithmetic will be done on a grid over
[-b,b]^{4Td}
with
N
grid points per dimension.

By Lemma
C.2
Lipschitzness of
f
, and Equation (
6.13
) we have

\displaystyle\max_{\vec{x}\in[-b,b]^{4Td}}\lvert f\circ h(\vec{y},\vec{z},\vec{x},\vec{w})\rvert=\mathcal{O}\left(BTde^{dT^{2}b^{3/2}}\right).

(D.3)

By Equation (
4.11
), we take

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\text{distr}}\mapsto\frac{\epsilon_{\text{distr}}}{4Td\max_{\vec{x}\in[-b,b]^{4Td}}\lvert f\circ h(\vec{y},\vec{z},\vec{x},\vec{w})\rvert}.

Hence all
\epsilon_{\text{distr}}
need to be scaled down by this, which we will handle latter.

Note that the fast-forwarding scheme for Heston requires that we first load a CIR processes. Specifically, we need to load
d
states of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{(k,j)\in\mathcal{M}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z}_{k})p_{\chi}(\vec{y}_{j})}|\vec{z}_{k}\rangle|\vec{y}_{j}\rangle|g_{1}(\vec{z}_{k},\vec{y}_{j})\rangle\cdots|g_{T}(\vec{z}_{k},\vec{y}_{j})\rangle.

This cost follows from the proof of Theorem
6.1
and the scaling of
\epsilon_{\textup{distr}}
presented above.

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(dT^{3}b^{3/2}\cdot Td(b+r)\log(N)\log(BTd/\epsilon_{\textup{distr}})+dT^{2}\log^{2}(N)\log(T)\right),

where
N=\Omega\left(Td\cdot BTdre^{dT^{2}b^{3/2}}/\epsilon_{\text{distr}}\right)
.

We then need to load
Td
more standard Gaussians over
N
grid points each, which can be done with a gate cost of

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(dT^{3}b^{3/2}\cdot Tdb\log(N)\log(BTd/\epsilon_{\textup{distr}})\right).

Then to load the integral over CIR, which we need to do
dT
times, we can apply Corollary
5.2.2
. Note that by our choice of truncation and Lemma
C.2
, we have
\max_{t}V(t)=\mathcal{O}(bT)
. Hence the gate cost to load the integral over CIR is

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(Td(bT)^{4}\log^{4}\left(bTN\right)\right).

The next step is to compute (
6.9
) into a register. This requires
\mathcal{O}\left(T\right)
additions/subtractions, multiplications/scalings and square-roots. There also an additional inner product computation for
d
-dimensional vectors. However, unlike CIR, there is not additional repeated square-rooting, beyond the CIR loading step.
This operations will be applied to roughly
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(N)\right)
numbers, indicating a
\mathcal{O}\left(Td^{2}\log^{2}(N)\right)

total gate cost for the basic arithmetic operations.

Then, we need to sum and exponentiate the results of the previous step. Since we are exponentiating, by (
D.3
), the number of output bits can be
\mathcal{O}(dT^{2}b^{3/2})
. Hence we can perform
dT
exponentials applied to the sum of the
U_{k}(t)
, using

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(dT\cdot(dT^{2}b^{3/2})^{2}\log(TdN)\right).

The last steps are to compute the payoff and rotate the result onto an amplitude. The rest now follows Lemma
B.2
, with
\Lambda=\mathcal{O}(BTde^{dT^{2}b^{3/2}})
. This gives
\mathcal{O}\left(\log^{3}(\Lambda N)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(T^{6}d^{3}b^{9/2}\right)
additional gates to perform the rotation onto an amplitude.

∎

D.3
Proof of Lemma
6.2.2

We have the following tail bound, which will be useful in bounding the truncation error. For conciseness, we will also interchange the notation
V(s)
with
V_{s}
.

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
V(s)
be a CIR process with initial condition
v_{0}
, parameters
\kappa,\sigma,\theta
, and step-size
\Delta\geq 1
. In addition suppose the process satisfies the Feller condition s.t. a.s.
V\succ\mathbf{0}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\int_{r}^{r+2\Delta}V(s)ds\geq x|V_{r},V_{r+2\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\Delta\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{2\kappa}\right)^{\xi+1}\exp(\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}\right]\frac{V_r + V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^2})\exp(-\frac{\kappa^2}{2\sigma^2}x).

where
\xi
is the Feller gap.

Proof.

Let
g(a)=\sqrt{\kappa^{2}-2a\sigma^{2}}
. Then the conditional MGF is
[
BK06
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(a\int_{r}^{r+2\Delta} V({s}) ds)|V(r),V({r+2\Delta})]

\displaystyle=\frac{g(a)\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\kappa\sinh(g(a)\Delta)}\exp\left(\frac{V_{r}+V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}-\frac{g(a)}{\tanh(g(a)\Delta)}\right)\right)\frac{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{r}V_{r+2\Delta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2g(a)}{\sinh(g(a)\Delta)}\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{r}V_{r+2\Delta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}.

The above is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(a\int_{r}^{r+2\Delta} V(s) ds)|V(r),V({r+2\Delta})]\leq\frac{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\kappa}\exp\left(\frac{V_{r}+V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}-\frac{g(a)}{\tanh(g(a)\Delta)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{I_{\xi}\left(2\frac{\sqrt{V_{r}V_{r+2\Delta}}}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{V_{r}V_{r+2\Delta}}}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{(x/2)^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}<I_{\nu}(x)<\frac{(x/2)^{\nu}e^{x}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)},

so

\displaystyle\frac{I_{\nu}(x)}{I_{\nu}(y)}<e^{x}(x/y)^{\nu}.

Consider
a=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}
, then
g(a)=0
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(a\int_{r}^{r+2\Delta} V(s) ds)|V(r),V({r+2\Delta})]

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{\Delta\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{2\kappa})^{\xi+1}\exp\left(\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh{\kappa\Delta}}-\Delta\right]\left(\frac{V_{r}+V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)+\frac{2\sqrt{V_{r}V_{r+2\Delta}}}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{\Delta\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{2\kappa})^{\xi+1}\exp\left(\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh{\kappa\Delta}}\right]\left(\frac{V_{r}+V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right).

Chernoff bounding gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\int_{r}^{r+2\Delta}V(s)ds\geq x|V_{r},V_{r+2\Delta}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Delta\frac{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\kappa}\right)^{\xi+1}\exp(\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}\right]\frac{V_r + V_{r+2\Delta}}{\sigma^2})\exp(-\frac{\kappa^2}{2\sigma^2}x).

∎

See
6.2.2

Proof.

Since we are assuming the payoff
f
is upper bounded by a
B
-Lipschitz linear function in all assets across all time points:

\displaystyle\lvert f(\widehat{S})\rvert\leq B\sum_{k,t}\widehat{S}_{k}(t),

which implies that we can without loss of generality look at the truncation bounds for a single
\widehat{S}_{k}(t)
. Thus, we will now drop the
k
subscript on
S
.

Due to the Feller condition, we can assume
\widehat{V}(t)\succeq 0
and thus
\vec{X}(t)\succeq 0
. Note then
\vec{z},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{T},\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)d},\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{T-1}
. We use
\vec{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to denote the entries from
td
to
(t+1)d
. Consider

\displaystyle\int s(t)p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})

\displaystyle\propto\int e^{\frac{\rho}{\sigma}(g_{t+1}(\vec{y},\vec{z}))+\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\Delta\right)\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{t}\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{t}}\cdot(\mathbf{A}_{k,\star}\vec{w}_{t})}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})

\displaystyle=\int e^{\frac{\rho}{\sigma}v_{t+1}+\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\Delta\right)\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}+\sum_{t}\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{t}}\cdot(\mathbf{A}_{k,\star}\vec{w}_{t})}p(\vec{x}|v_{t+1},v_{0})p(v_{t+1}|v_{0})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w}).

The equality follows by performing a change of variables using

\displaystyle(z_{t},y_{t})\rightarrow(z_{t},c(y_{t}+(z_{t}+\sqrt{\beta g_{t-1}})^{2}))=:(z_{t},v_{t}),

where
(\vec{z},\vec{y})\rightarrow(\vec{y},\vec{v})
can be seen to be invertible. More specifically,

\displaystyle p_{z,y}(\vec{z},\vec{y})d\vec{z}d\vec{y}

\displaystyle=p_{z,y}(\vec{z},\vec{y}(\vec{z},\vec{v}))\frac{\partial(\vec{z},\vec{y})}{\partial(\vec{z},\vec{v})}d\vec{z}d\vec{v}=p_{z,v}(\vec{z},\vec{v})d\vec{z}d\vec{v}.

Then we marginalize out the
\vec{z}
and
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
.
We use proportionality since some constant factors are dropped for now.

Let us start with the innermost Gaussian integral over
\vec{w}
. Recall that
\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)d}
, where the
w_{m,r}
will denote the
m
-th time step for the
r
-th asset. Also,
\vec{w}_{m}
denotes the vector of
m
-th time step increments for all assets.

Suppose we integrate over
\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}}p(\vec{w})\exp(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^2)x_{m}} \cdot(\mathbf{A}_{k, \star}\cdot\vec{w}_{m}))

\displaystyle=\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}}\mathcal{C}\exp\left(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}w_{m,r}^{2}+\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{m}}(A_{k,r}\cdot w_{m,r})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}}\mathcal{C}\exp\left(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{m,r}-\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{m}}A_{k,r}\right)^{2}\right)\exp\left(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}(A_{k,r})^{2}[\Delta(1-\rho^{2})]x_{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}}\mathcal{C}\exp\left(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}-\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{m,r}-\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{m}}A_{k,r}\right)^{2}\right)\exp\left([\Delta(1-\rho^{2})]\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}}\mathcal{C}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\lVert\vec{w}-\vec{\mu}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\exp\left([\Delta(1-\rho^{2})]\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}\right),

where we used that since
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{A}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}
is a correlation matrix. Also
\mu_{k,r}=\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{m}}A_{k,r}
. From Gaussian concentration bounds we get that

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}+\sqrt{dT}\log(1/\epsilon)\right)

suffices for an
\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon\right)
error. From the above, we also have the following upper bound on the integral over the whole domain:

\displaystyle\int_{\lVert\vec{w}\rVert_{2}\geq 0}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})\exp(\sum_{m=0}^{t-2}\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^2)x_{m}} \cdot(\mathbf{A}_{k, \star}\cdot\vec{w}_{m}))\leq\exp\left(\Delta(1-\rho^{2})\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}\right).

(D.4)

Next we bound the integral w.r.t.
\vec{x}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}\geq\alpha_{3}}e^{\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right)\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1}}p(\vec{x}|v_{t+1},v_{0})d\vec{x}.

Recall
\vec{x}\succ 0
, so given the invertible map :

\vec{x}\rightarrow(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{t-2},\lVert\vec{x}\rVert_{1})=:(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{t-1},x)
, we perform a change of variables and marginalize out
x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{t-2}
:

\displaystyle\int_{x\geq\alpha_{3}}e^{\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right)x}p(x|v_{t+1},v_{0})dx=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right)\alpha_{3}}\mathbb{P}[x\geq\alpha_{3}|v_{t+1},v_{0}]\right),

where
X
is clearly distributed as
\int_{0}^{t+1}V(2\Delta s)ds
. The equality follows from Lemma
F
. We can then apply Lemma
D.3
via:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{t+1}V(2\Delta s)ds\geq x|\widehat{V}(t+1),\widehat{V}(0)]=\mathbb{P}[\int_{0}^{2\Delta(t+1)}V(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\geq 2\Delta x|V(2\Delta(t+1)),V(0)].

We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[x\geq\alpha_{3}|v_{t+1},v_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq\left((t+1)\Delta\frac{\sinh(\kappa(t+1)\Delta)}{2\kappa}\right)^{\xi+1}\exp\left(\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa(t+1)\Delta)}\right]\frac{v_{t+1}+v_{0}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\kappa^{2}\Delta}{\sigma^{2}}\alpha_{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{(t+1)\Delta}{2\kappa}\right)^{\xi+1}\exp\left(\kappa(t+1)(\xi+1)\Delta+\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa(t+1)\Delta)}\right]\frac{v_{t+1}+v_{0}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\kappa^{2}\Delta}{\sigma^{2}}\alpha_{3}\right).

Hence Lemma
D.3
is valid if

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}>2\kappa\sigma\rho-\rho^{2}\sigma^{2}\geq 0.

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{x\geq\alpha_{3}}e^{\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right)x}p(x|v_{t+1},v_{0})dx

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(\left(\frac{(t+1)\Delta}{2\kappa}\right)^{\xi+1}\exp\left(\Delta(t+1)(\xi+1)+\left[\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa(t+1)\Delta)}\right]\frac{v_{t+1}+v_{0}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\exp\left(\left[-\frac{\kappa^{2}\Delta}{\sigma^{2}}+\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right]\alpha_{3}\right)\bigg).

From a calculation below, it will be apparent that the integral of the above w.r.t.
v_{t+1}
will
\mathcal{O}(1)
, so we will be left with

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{x\geq\alpha_{3}}e^{\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\Delta\right)x}p(x|v_{t+1},v_{0})dxdv_{t+1}=\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{t\Delta}{\kappa}\right)^{\xi}e^{\Delta\xi t-\left[\frac{\kappa^{2}\Delta}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}+\rho^{2}\Delta\right]\alpha_{3}}\right)

so

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}=\frac{T\xi\sigma^{2}\ln(T\Delta/\epsilon\kappa)}{(\kappa^{2}-2\kappa\sigma\rho+\rho\sigma^{2})}

suffices for
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
error.

Next we bound the integral w.r.t.
\vec{v}
(dropping some constant factors depending on the initial condition). We also undo the change of variables to bring back the dependence on
\vec{y}
and
\vec{z}
.

\displaystyle\int_{\lVert\vec{z}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}\geq\alpha_{1}}p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p(\vec{z})\exp(\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta(t+1)\kappa) + \rho\sigma}{\sigma^2}g_{t+1}(\vec{z}, \vec{y}))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\lVert\vec{z}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}\geq\alpha_{1}}p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})\exp((1+\gamma)c\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta(t+1)\kappa) + \rho\sigma}{\sigma^2}(\lVert\vec{z} \rVert_2^2 + \lVert\vec{y}\rVert_1))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{Y}\geq\alpha_{1}}p(\mathcal{Y})\exp((1+\gamma)c\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta(t+1)\kappa) + \rho\sigma}{\sigma^2}\mathcal{Y})

where we used Lemma
C.2
to bound
g_{t+1}
.
Lastly, recall that the components of
\vec{Y}
are i.i.d.
\chi^{2}_{\eta-1}
and
\vec{z}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussian. Thus
\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1}
is
\chi^{2}_{t\eta}
distributed leading to the change of variables to
\mathcal{Y}\sim\chi^{2}_{t\eta}
. Specifically,
(y_{1},\dots y_{t+1},\vec{z})\rightarrow(\lVert\vec{z}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lVert\vec{y}\rVert_{1},y_{2},\dots y_{t},\vec{z})
, which is invertible since
\vec{y}\succeq 0
.

Note that by Chernoff bounding and using Lemma
F
for
\lambda=\frac{1}{4}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{Y}\geq b]\leq e^{-\lambda b}(1-2\lambda)^{-r/2}\leq e^{-\frac{b}{4}+\frac{r}{2}\ln(2)}.

Define
m=c(1+\gamma)\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta(t+1)\kappa)+\rho\sigma}{\sigma^{2}}
, clearly we need
m<1/2
. Then let
r=t\eta

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Y}\geq\alpha_{1}}p(\mathcal{Y})\exp(m\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle=(1-2m)^{-r/2}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{Y}\geq(1-2m)\alpha_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq(1-2m)^{-r/2}e^{-\frac{(1-2m)\alpha_{1}}{4}+\frac{r}{2}\ln(2)},

implies we can select
\alpha_{1}=\mathcal{O}\left(r\ln(1/\epsilon)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(T\ln(1/\epsilon)\right)
.

To ensure the overall error is
\epsilon
we apply a union bound and need to scale down the
\epsilon
’s by
\mathcal{O}(Td)
.
Hence

\displaystyle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}=\mathcal{O}(T^{2}\ln(Td/\epsilon))

(D.5)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}=\mathcal{O}(T^{3/2}\sqrt{d}\log(Td/\epsilon)).

(D.6)

There is an additional factor of
T
from the
e^{T\mu}
prefactor in the definition of
\widehat{S}
, which requires scaling down
\epsilon
.
Thus it suffices to truncate
\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)d},\vec{y},\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{T},\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{T-1}
to
\ell_{\infty}
balls of size
\mathcal{O}\left((T^{3/2}\sqrt{d}+T^{2})\log(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})\right)
.

Note that

\displaystyle c\frac{\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}+\rho\sigma}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-2\kappa\Delta}}{4\kappa}\cdot\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}+\rho\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+e^{-2\kappa\Delta}}{4}+\frac{\rho\sigma}{\kappa}\frac{1-e^{-2\kappa\Delta}}{4},

which we need to be less than
\frac{1}{2(1+\gamma)}=\frac{1}{2(1+e^{-\kappa\Delta/2})}
.

∎

D.4
Proof of Theorem
6.5

We start by analyzing the error incurred from leaving out an interval around
0
for
\vec{x}_{r}
. The reason for doing this is that for the discretization error, the upper bound we have on the partial derivative is upper bounded by
\frac{1}{\alpha}
for
\vec{x}_{r}\geq\alpha
. Since the number of qubits depends logarithmically on this upper bound, the poor dependence on the cutoff point is not a problem.

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Suppose the conditions of Lemma
6.2.2
are satisfied. If we truncate all
X
to an
\ell_{\infty}
ball of size

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left((\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}/Td)^{T^{3}d+1}\right)

around zero, then the truncation error is only
\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon\right)
.

Proof.

Note that the probability that the continuous-time process
V_{s}
hits the set
[0,\alpha]
within the time interval
[0,\Delta T]
is known given the Feller condition (
\xi>0
) is satisfied
[
Gik11
]
. Specifically, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\min_{s\in[0,T-1]}V_{s}\leq\alpha|V_{0}=v_{0}]\leq(v_{0}e^{\Delta T}\alpha)^{\xi},

(D.7)

where we clearly need
V_{0}>\alpha
. Note that this inequality clarifies the role of the Feller condition, it identifies a phase transition at which
\mathbb{P}[\min_{s\in[0,T-1]}V_{s}\leq\alpha]\rightarrow 0
as
\alpha\rightarrow 0
.

From the previous Lemma, we can consider integrating
\vec{w},\vec{y},\vec{z},\vec{x}
over a bounded domain. The following will bound the additional error that comes from leaving out a small ball of
\vec{x}
near zero. The triangle inequality bounds the overall truncation error.

Let
\alpha_{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
m=\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\kappa)+\rho\sigma}{\sigma^{2}}
and some arbitrary
t>s
. Let
x_{[a,b]}:=\sum_{k=a}^{b}x_{k}
. The following drops some irrelevant constant factors. From the previous proof and Equation (
D.4
) we know that

\displaystyle\int_{x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}}s(t)p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})\leq\int_{x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}}p(v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1}|v_{0})p(\vec{x}|v_{0},v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1})e^{\frac{\rho}{\sigma}v_{t+1}+\Delta\left(\frac{2\kappa\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\right)\sum_{t}x_{t}}

\displaystyle=\int p(v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1}|v_{0})p(x_{[0,s-1]},x_{[s+1,t]}|v_{0},v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1})e^{\frac{\rho}{\sigma}v_{t+1}+\Delta\left(\frac{2\kappa\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\right)\sum_{k\neq s}x_{k}}\int_{x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}}p(x_{s}|v_{s},v_{s+1})e^{\Delta\left(\frac{2\kappa\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\right)x_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\int p(v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1}|v_{0})p(x_{[0,s-1]},x_{[s+1,t]}|v_{0},v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1})e^{\frac{\rho}{\sigma}v_{t+1}+\Delta\left(\frac{2\kappa\rho}{\sigma}-\rho^{2}\right)(x_{[0,s-1]}+x_{[s+1,t]})}\mathbb{P}[x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s},v_{s+1}]

\displaystyle\leq\int p(v_{s},v_{s+1},v_{t+1}|v_{0})e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})+mv_{t+1}}\mathbb{P}[x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s},v_{s+1}]

\displaystyle\leq\int p(v_{s},v_{s+1}|v_{0})e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s},v_{s+1}]\int p(v_{t+1}|v_{s+1})e^{mv_{t+1}}.

The fourth line follows from
x_{s}=\int_{s}^{s+1}v_{r}dr\geq\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}
. Note that some irrelevant constant factors have been dropped.

We want to marginalize out the
v_{s},v_{s+1},
and
v_{t+1}
and can use the following trick. Recall that
V(t)
conditioned on
V(s)
is noncentral
\chi^{2}
distributed:

\displaystyle\frac{V(t)}{c}

\displaystyle=(Y(t)+(Z(t)+\gamma\sqrt{\frac{V(s)}{c}})^{2})

\displaystyle\implies V(t)\leq c(1+\gamma)(Y(t)+Z(t)^{2})+\gamma(1+\gamma)V(s),

by Jensen’s inequality and where
Y
is
\chi^{2}_{\xi}
and
Z
standard Gaussian.

For arbitrary
v_{s},v_{t}
and
b
, then

\displaystyle\int p(v_{t}|v_{s})e^{bv_{t}}dv_{t}

\displaystyle=\int p(y,z)e^{c(y+(z+\sqrt{\beta v_{s}})^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq e^{(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s}}\int p(y,z)e^{bc(1+\gamma)(y+z^{2})}

\displaystyle=e^{(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s}}\int p(y+z^{2})e^{bc(1+\gamma)(y+z^{2})},

where
\mathcal{Y}:=Y+Z^{2}
is
\chi^{2}_{\eta}
distributed. We need
b^{\prime}=bc(1+\gamma)<1/2
.

Thus from Lemma
F

\displaystyle\int p(v_{t}|v_{s})e^{bv_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s}}(1-2b^{\prime})^{-\eta/2}\int p((1-2b^{\prime})\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle\leq e^{(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s}}(1-2b^{\prime})^{-\eta/2}.

If we apply this to the expression above

\displaystyle\int p(v_{t+1}|v_{s+1})e^{mv_{t+1}}\leq e^{(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s+1}}(1-2c(1+\gamma)m)^{-\eta/2},

where
c(1+\gamma)m<\frac{1}{2}
is implied by the fourth condition in Lemma
6.2.2
.

Then we apply Hölder’s inequality to get

\displaystyle\int p(v_{s+1}|v_{s})e^{\left(\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\right)v_{s+1}}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s},v_{s+1}]dv_{s+1}

\displaystyle\leq\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{s})}e^{\left(\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\right)v_{s+1}}\cdot\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s}],

and again

\displaystyle\int p(v_{s}|v_{0})\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{s})}e^{\left(\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\right)v_{s+1}}e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}v_{s}}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{s}]

\displaystyle\leq\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{0})}e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s+1}}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{0}].

Recall, we can assume
v_{s},v_{s+1}
are bounded (we assume the upper truncation has already been applied). Hence, the overall bound is

\displaystyle\int_{x_{s}\leq\alpha_{4}}s(t)p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-2c(1+\gamma)m\right)^{-\eta/2}\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{0})}e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s+1}}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[s,s+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-2c(1+\gamma)m\right)^{-\eta/2}\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{0})}e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s+1}}\mathbb{P}[\min_{r\in[0,t+1]}v_{r}\leq\alpha_{4}|v_{0}]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-2c(1+\gamma)m\right)^{-\eta/2}\text{ess sup}_{p(\cdot|v_{0})}e^{\frac{\kappa/\tanh(\Delta\kappa)}{\sigma^{2}}(v_{s}+v_{s+1})+(\gamma+\gamma^{2})v_{s+1}}(v_{0}e^{\Delta(t+1)}\alpha_{4})^{\xi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left((Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d}\alpha_{4}\right),

which implies
\alpha_{4}=\mathcal{O}\left((\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}/Td)^{T^{3}d+1}\right)

suffices. We use that
Z,Y
are truncated to
\ell_{\infty}
balls of size
\mathcal{O}(T^{2}d\log(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}))
and hence
V(t)
to
\mathcal{O}(T^{3}d\log(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}}))
.
∎

See
6.5

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{*}
be
\mathcal{O}
but ignore factors that are polynomial in
T
or
d
or
\log
in the inverse error. Since the number of bits is logarithmic in the derivatives, we only care about factors that are exponential in
T
or
d
or polynomial in inverse error.

We continue to use the notation from Section
6.2
. Hence we have four discrete-time processes:
\vec{Z},\vec{Y}
are length
T
, and
\vec{X},\vec{W}
are length
T-1
. The path process
\widehat{S}
is length
T
and
\widehat{V}
is length
T+1
. The function
h_{t}
computes the
t
-th point in
\widehat{S}
from the vector of path increments.

Since the payoff
f
is piecewise linear with slope at most
B
, we can without loss of generality, apply the discretization analysis to the function
B\sum_{t,k}h_{t}^{(k)}
. Hence, we simply denote
h^{(k)}_{t}
the function that produces the
k
asset at the
t
time step for arbitrary
t
and
k
. Since
k
is arbitrary, for conciseness, we will drop the
k
superscript on
h
. Specifically,

\displaystyle u_{t}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})=2\Delta\mu t-2\kappa\theta\Delta\frac{\rho}{\sigma}+\frac{\rho}{\sigma}(g_{t+1}(\vec{y},\vec{z})-g_{t}(\vec{y},\vec{z}))+\left(\frac{2\kappa\Delta\rho}{\sigma}-\Delta\right)x_{t}+\sqrt{2\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{t}}\cdot(\mathbf{C}_{k,*}\vec{w})

\displaystyle h_{t}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})=\widehat{S}_{k}(0)\exp\left(\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}u_{t}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{t}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})=h_{t}(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})

The desired error will then be scaled down by
\Theta(BTd)
to ensure the overall error for
B\sum_{t,k}h_{t}^{(k)}
is sufficiently small.

The truncated price integral is over a
m:=2dT+2d(T-1)
-dimensional hyper-rectangle:

\displaystyle(\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w})\in[-\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}]^{dT}\times[0,\alpha_{1}]^{dT}\times[\alpha_{4},\alpha_{3}]^{d(T-1)}\times[-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}]^{d(T-1)}=:\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Let
R=\max(2\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{4},2\alpha_{2})
. We let
\mathcal{M}
denote a uniform grid over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
with spacing
\frac{R}{N}
per dimension.

The discretization error from Lemma
F
is

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{R}_{N}(\widehat{h})-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\widehat{h}(\vec{r})d\vec{r}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left[\sum_{\vec{r}\in{\mathcal{M}}}\sup_{\vec{j}\in C_{\vec{r}}}\lVert\nabla\widehat{h}(\vec{j})\rVert_{\infty}\left(\frac{R}{N}\right)^{m}\right]\frac{8dTR}{N}

(D.8)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\vec{j}\in{\mathcal{M}}}\lVert\nabla\widehat{h}(\vec{j})\rVert_{\infty}\frac{8dTR^{m+1}}{N}.

(D.9)

Note that we choose to utilize a uniform bound on
\nabla h
over the whole box, unlike the more refined estimates we used for CIR and GBM. This is because we do not know the pdf of
\vec{X}
in closed formed, which makes it challenging to achieve the relative-error bounds we got for GBM. This leads to a bit scaling that grows with dimension, which is likely overly-pessimistic. Due to this bound, we will only need to track factors that are exponential in
d
or
T
.

Next, we need to bound the partial derivatives
h_{t}
with respect to
\vec{z},\vec{y},\vec{x},\vec{w}
. Since we only care about asymptotics, we will ignore constant factors.

1.

y_{v}
:
\partial_{y_{v}}\widehat{h}_{t}\lesssim h_{t}\left[\sum_{r=v}^{t-1}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})\partial_{y_{v}}u_{r}+\partial_{y_{v}}p_{\chi}(\vec{y})+\partial_{y_{v}}p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\right]

2.

z_{v}
:
\partial_{z_{v}}\widehat{h}_{t}\lesssim h_{t}\left[\sum_{r=v}^{t-1}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})\partial_{z_{v}}u_{r}+\partial_{z_{v}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})+\partial_{z_{v}}p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\right]

3.

x_{v}
:
\partial_{x_{v}}\widehat{h}_{t}\lesssim h_{t}\left[\partial_{x_{v}}u_{v}+\partial_{x_{v}}p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\right]

4.

w_{v}
:
\partial_{w_{v}}\widehat{h}_{t}\lesssim h_{t}\left[\partial_{w_{v}}u_{v}+\partial_{w_{v}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{w})\right],

where
\lesssim
here is used to indicate asymptotic comparison.

Recall that

\displaystyle\Phi(a)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\gamma_{a}\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}{\kappa\sinh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\exp\left(\frac{v_{t}+v_{t+2\Delta}}{\sigma^{2}}\cdot\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}-\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\right)\right)\frac{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{v_{t}v_{t+1}}}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{\sqrt{v_{t}v_{t+1}}}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)},

(D.10)

and so

\displaystyle p_{X}(x_{v}|\vec{z},\vec{y})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{M}(e^{-iax_{v}}\Phi(a)+e^{iax_{v}}\Phi^{*}(a))da.

(D.11)

First we start by bounding
\lvert\partial_{s_{v}}p_{X}(\cdot|\vec{z},\vec{y})\rvert
for
s_{v}=y_{v}
or
z_{v}
. Recall that
v_{t}
can be computed from
\vec{z}
and
\vec{y}
through
g_{t}
.

Consider

\displaystyle\lvert\frac{d}{dq}\frac{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\right)}{I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)}\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\left[I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)\right]^{-2}\frac{2\gamma_{a}}{\sigma^{2}\sinh(\gamma_a\Delta)}I_{\xi}^{\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\right)I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)\right]^{-2}\frac{2\kappa}{\sigma^{2}\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}I_{\xi}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}\right)I_{\xi}^{\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{2\kappa}{\sinh(\kappa\Delta)}\right)\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*}\left(e^{\frac{8q}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}}\right),

where the last equality follows from

\displaystyle I_{\xi}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(I_{\xi-1}(z)+I_{\xi+1}(z)\right)

(D.12)

\displaystyle\frac{(x/2)^{\xi}}{\Gamma(\xi+1)}<I_{\xi}(x)<\frac{(x/2)^{\xi}e^{x}}{\Gamma(\xi+1)}

(D.13)

\displaystyle\lvert\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\sinh(\gamma_a\Delta)}\rvert\leq\frac{2}{\Delta}.

(D.14)

We have that
q=\sqrt{v_{t+1}v_{t}}
, so we apply chain rule for
s_{v}=y_{v}
or
s_{v}=z_{v}
:

\displaystyle\frac{v_{t+1}\partial_{s_{v}}v_{t}+v_{t}\partial_{s_{v}}v_{t+1}}{2\sqrt{v_{t}v_{t+1}}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\partial_{s_{v}}v_{t}}{2\sqrt{v_{t}}}\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})+\frac{\partial_{s_{v}}v_{t+1}}{2\sqrt{v_{t+1}}}\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y}),

where
\alpha_{1}
is our truncation bound on
\vec{v}
from the proof of Lemma
6.2.2
.
Since
\sqrt{v_{t}}=\sqrt{g_{t}}\geq\sqrt{y_{t-1}}
we get that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{s_{j}}v_{t}}{\sqrt{v_{t}}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})

\displaystyle=\partial_{s_{j}}v_{j}\frac{\partial_{v_{j}}v_{t}}{\sqrt{v_{t}}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{s_{j}}v_{j}\frac{\partial_{v_{j}}v_{t}}{\sqrt{y_{t-1}}}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{s_{j}}v_{j}\frac{1}{\sqrt{y_{t-1}}}\prod_{k=j}^{t-1}\frac{z_{k}y_{k-1}^{r/2-3/2}+y_{k-1}^{r/2-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}e^{-\frac{y_{k-1}+z_{k}^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\partial_{s_{j}}v_{j}\frac{y_{t-1}^{r/2-3/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}e^{-\frac{y_{k-1}}{2}}\prod_{k=j}^{t-1}\frac{z_{k}y_{k-1}^{r/2-3/2}+y_{k-1}^{r/2-1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\Gamma(r/2)2^{r/2}}e^{-\frac{y_{k-1}+z_{k}^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*}(1),

which follows from Equation (
C.2
). Hence
\frac{\partial q}{\partial s_{v}}=\mathcal{O}^{*}(1)
.

The above then implies that

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{s_{v}}p_{X}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq M\cdot\max_{a\in[0,M]}\lvert\partial_{s_{v}}\Phi(a)\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*}\left(\exp\left(\frac{v_{t}+v_{t+1}}{\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\tanh(\kappa\Delta)}-\text{Re}[\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\tanh(\gamma_{a}\Delta)}]\right)+\frac{8}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{v_{t}v_{t+1}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{*}\left(\exp\left(\frac{8\alpha_{1}(\kappa+1)}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}\right)\right).

Thus combining the above results, we get that
\lvert\partial_{s_{v}}p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\rvert=\mathcal{O}^{*}\left(\exp\left(\frac{8\alpha_{1}(\kappa+1)}{\Delta\sigma^{2}}\right)\right)
.

Next, we bound
\lvert\partial_{x_{v}}p_{X}(x_{v}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\rvert
. From Equation (
D.11
), we get that
\lvert\partial_{x_{v}}p_{X}(x_{v}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\rvert\leq M\cdot\max_{a\in[0,M]}\Phi(a).
Thus, due to form of
\Phi(a)
,
\lvert\partial_{x_{v}}p_{X}(x_{v}|\vec{z},\vec{y})\rvert
is
\mathcal{O}^{*}
of the same quantity as
\partial_{s_{v}}p_{X}(\vec{x}|\vec{z},\vec{y})
.

From the proof of Theorem
6.2
, we know that
\partial_{y_{v}}p_{\chi}(\vec{y})=\mathcal{O}(1)
, and
\partial_{z}p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Next, we bound

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})\partial_{s_{v}}u_{r},

for
s_{v}
equal to
y_{v},z_{v},w_{v}
or
x_{v}
. Note that only the first term in Equation (
6.10
) depends on
y_{v},z_{v}
, which is just a linear combination of
p_{\mathcal{N}}(\vec{z})p_{\chi}(\vec{y})\partial_{s_{v}}v_{r}
terms. From the proof of Theorem
6.2
, we know that these terms are
\mathcal{O}^{*}(1)
. For
\partial_{w_{v}}u_{v}
, we have a bound of

\displaystyle A_{k,v}\cdot\sqrt{\Delta(1-\rho^{2})x_{v}}\leq A_{k,v}\cdot\sqrt{\Delta(1-\rho^{2})\alpha_{3}}=\mathcal{O}^{*}(1),

and for
\partial_{x_{v}}u_{v}
we have a bound of

\displaystyle A_{k,\cdot}\vec{w}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\Delta(1-\rho^{2})}}{\sqrt{x_{v}}}=\mathcal{O}^{*}(1/\alpha_{4})=\mathcal{O}^{*}((Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d+1}),

using the value from Lemma
D.4
.

Note that the additional exponential factor in
\nabla h
is
\exp\left(\sum_{r=0}^{t-1}u_{r}\right)=\mathcal{O}^{\star}\left(e^{T(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{2}\sqrt{\alpha_{3}})}\right)
. If we include this with our bounds on the various components of
\nabla\ln(h)
, we get from (
D.5
) and (
D.6
):

\displaystyle\max_{\mathcal{M}}\lVert\nabla h\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{\star}\left(e^{T(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{2}\sqrt{\alpha_{3}})}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{\star}\left(e^{T^{5/2}d^{1/2}\log^{3/2}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d+1}\right)

where summing over all assets at each time step does not change
\mathcal{O}^{*}
complexity. Thus combining this bound with Equation (
D.9
) gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{\star}\left(e^{T^{5/2}d^{1/2}\log^{3/2}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d}R^{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}^{\star}\left(e^{T^{5/2}d^{1/2}\log^{3/2}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})^{T^{3}d}\left[(T^{2}+T^{3/2}\sqrt{d})\log(1/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})\right]^{4Td}\right).

So it suffices to take

\displaystyle\log_{2}(N)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(T^{3}d\log^{3/2}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})\log(Td/\epsilon_{\text{disc}})\right),

and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(T^{4}d^{2}\log^{3/2}(Td/\epsilon_{\text{trunc}})\log(Td/\epsilon_{\text{disc}})\right)

(qu)bits in total.

∎

Appendix E
Additional Proofs for Section
3.1.4
&
7

E.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Theorem E.1
.

Let
P
denote a random variable, and let
P_{l}(l=0,1,\dots,L)
denote a sequence of random variables such that
P_{l}
approximates
P
at level
l
.
Let
\hat{Y}_{l}
denote the unbiased estimator for
P_{l}-P_{l-1}
constructed from
N_{l}
samples of
P_{l}-P_{l-1}
, where we define
P_{-1}\equiv 0
.
Let
V_{l}
and
C_{l}
be the variance and computational complexity of
\hat{Y}_{l}
respectively.
If there exists positive constants
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
,
\delta
and
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
c_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\mathbb{E}\left[ P_l - P \right]}\leq c_{1}h_{l}^{\alpha},

(E.1)

\displaystyle V[\hat{Y}_{l}]\leq c_{2}N_{l}^{-\delta}h_{l}^{\beta},

(E.2)

\displaystyle C_{l}\leq c_{3}N_{l}h_{l}^{-\gamma},

(E.3)

where
h_{l}=M^{-l}T
and
M>1
is an integer,
then for any
\epsilon<1
, there is an algorithm that estimates
\mathbb{E}[P]
up to a mean-squared error
\epsilon^{2}
with a computational complexity
C
bounded by

\begin{cases}O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right),&\gamma<\frac{\beta}{\delta},\\
O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}+1}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right),&\gamma=\frac{\beta}{\delta},\\
O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}-\frac{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}{\alpha}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\delta}\left(2-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right),&\gamma>\frac{\beta}{\delta}.\end{cases}

(E.4)

Proof.

Let
\hat{Y}=\sum_{l=0}^{L}\hat{Y}_{l}
, and choose
L
to be

L=\left\lceil\frac{\log\left(\sqrt{2}c_{1}T^{\alpha}\epsilon^{-1}\right)}{\alpha\log M}\right\rceil,

(E.5)

so that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}M^{-\alpha}\epsilon<c_{1}h_{L}^{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\epsilon,

(E.6)

we will have

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{l=0}^{L}\mathbb{E}[\hat{Y}_{l}]-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}

(E.7)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{l=0}^{L}\mathbb{E}[P_{l}-P_{l-1}]-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}

(E.8)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{E}[P_{L}]-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}

(E.9)

\displaystyle\leq\left(c_{1}h_{L}^{\alpha}\right)^{2}

(E.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}.

(E.11)

If we also choose
N_{l}
to be

N_{l}=\left\lceil\left(\frac{1}{2c_{2}(L+1)}\epsilon^{2}h_{l}^{-\beta}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}\right\rceil,

(E.12)

then we will have

\mathbb{V}[\hat{Y}]=\sum_{l=1}^{L}c_{2}N_{l}^{-\delta}h_{l}^{\beta}\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2},

(E.13)

and consequently,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\hat{Y}-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{V}[\hat{Y}]+\left(\mathbb{E}[\hat{Y}]-\mathbb{E}[P]\right)^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2},

(E.14)

and hence we can use
\hat{Y}
as the estimator that satisfies the requirements in the theorem.

And the total computational complexity for
\hat{Y}
is

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{L}C_{l}\leq c_{3}\sum_{l=0}^{L}N_{l}h_{l}^{-\gamma}

(E.15)

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\sum_{l=0}^{L}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2c_{2}(L+1)}\epsilon^{2}h_{l}^{-\beta}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\delta}}+1\right]h_{l}^{-\gamma}

(E.16)

\displaystyle=c_{3}[2c_{2}(L+1)]^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}+c_{3}\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\gamma}.

(E.17)

From
Equation

E.6
we have

h_{L}^{\alpha}>\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}c_{1}}M^{-\alpha}\epsilon,

(E.18)

and hence

\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-d}=h_{L}^{-d}\sum_{l=0}^{L}M^{-dl}<\frac{M^{d}}{M^{d}-1}h_{L}^{-d}<\frac{M^{2d}}{M^{d}-1}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}c_{1}}\right)^{-\frac{d}{\alpha}},\qquad\forall\,d>0.

(E.19)

Therefore, the second term in
Equation

E.17
is bounded by

c_{3}\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\gamma}<c_{3}\frac{M^{2\gamma}}{M^{\gamma}-1}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}c_{1}}\right)^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right).

(E.20)

Now we prove the bounds on the first term in the computational complexity for
\hat{Y}
(
Equation

E.17
) for different cases.

(a) If
\gamma<\frac{\beta}{\delta}
,

\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}<\frac{1}{1-h_{l}^{\left(\frac{\beta}{\delta}-\gamma\right)}}=\frac{M^{\left(\frac{\beta}{\delta}-\gamma\right)}}{M^{\left(\frac{\beta}{\delta}-\gamma\right)}-1}T^{\left(\frac{\beta}{\delta}-\gamma\right)}=O(1).

(E.21)

In addition, from
Equation

E.5
we have
L=O(\log\epsilon^{-1})
.
Therefore

c_{3}[2c_{2}(L+1)]^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right),

(E.22)

and hence

C=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right).

(E.23)

(b) If
\gamma=\frac{\beta}{\delta}
,

\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}=L+1=O\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right).

(E.24)

Therefore, similar as the previous case,

C=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}+1}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right).

(E.25)

(c) If
\gamma>\frac{\beta}{\delta}
,

from
Equation

E.6
we have,

\sum_{l=0}^{L}h_{l}^{-\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}<\frac{M^{2\left(\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}\right)}}{M^{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}-1}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}c_{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}{\alpha}}=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}{\alpha}}\right).

(E.26)

Therefore

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}-\frac{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}{\alpha}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\right)

(E.27)

\displaystyle=O\left(\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\delta}-\frac{\gamma-\frac{\beta}{\delta}}{\alpha}}\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\right).

(E.28)

The second equality above is due to
\beta<2\alpha
.
∎

E.2
Derivation of Equation (
7.2
)

Recall the notation from Section
7.2
. We start by providing the bound on the variance for the truncated and discretized MLMC estimator. Let
p_{\ell}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times 2^{\ell}T}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the density for
\widetilde{Y}_{\ell}
and
p
the density for
\widehat{Y}
.

\displaystyle\textup{Var}(f(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell})-f(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell-1}))

\displaystyle\leq B^{2}T\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-\mathcal{Y}_{\ell-1}(t2^{\ell})\rVert_{2}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(B^{2}T\bigg(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\lvert\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-g_{\ell}(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell}))\rVert_{2}^{2}]-\int_{[-R,R]^{d\times 2^{\ell}T}}\lVert\vec{x}_{t2^{\ell}}-[g_{\ell}(\vec{x})]_{t2^{\ell}}\rVert_{2}^{2}p_{\ell}(\vec{x})\rvert

\displaystyle+\int_{[-R,R]^{d\times 2^{\ell}T}}\lVert\vec{x}_{t2^{\ell}}-[g(\vec{x})]_{t2^{\ell}}\rVert_{2}^{2}p_{\ell}(\vec{x})\rvert\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(B^{2}T\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\bigg(\lvert\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-g_{\ell}(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell}))\rVert_{2}^{2}]-\int_{[-R,R]^{d\times 2^{\ell}T}}\lVert\vec{x}_{t2^{\ell}}-[g_{\ell}(\vec{x})]_{t2^{\ell}}\rVert_{2}^{2}p_{\ell}(\vec{x})\rvert

\displaystyle+\int_{[-R,R]^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\lVert\vec{x}-\vec{y}\rVert_{2}^{2}\widetilde{p}_{\ell}(\vec{x})p(y)\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\bigg(B^{2}T\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\bigg(\lvert\mathbb{E}\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-g_{\ell}(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell}))\rVert_{2}^{2}-\int_{[-R,R]^{d\times 2^{\ell}T}}\lVert\vec{x}-g_{\ell}(\vec{x})\rVert_{2}^{2}p_{\ell}(\vec{x})\rvert

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[\lVert\widetilde{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-\widehat{Y}(t2^{\ell})\rVert_{2}^{2}]\bigg)\bigg).

The last equality follows from the positivity of the integrand. Note that
\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell})-g_{\ell}(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell}(t2^{\ell}))\rVert_{2}^{2}]
can be viewed as a discretized and renormalized approximation to the
truncated
expectation of the function
\lVert(i-g_{\ell})(\vec{x})\rVert_{2}^{2}
applied to a random variable with density
p_{\ell}(\vec{x})
, where
i(\vec{x})=\vec{x}
is the identity function. It is clear that
g_{\ell}
is
1
-Lipschitz, where the only non-Lipschitz component comes from the
\ell_{2}
norm.
Hence the second term corresponding to
\textup{Err}(\ell,t)
from the main text is just discretization error.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
7.3

See
7.3

Proof.

Since the function
g
in Equation (
7.4
) corresponds to
(n,2)
-Milstein sampling, we can without loss of generality assume each component of the
d
-dimensional SDE (Equation (
2.1
)) is of the form:

\displaystyle dX_{i}(t)=\mu_{i}(\vec{X}(t))dt+\sigma_{ii}(\vec{X}(t))dW_{i}+\sigma_{ij}(\vec{X}(t))dW_{j},

(E.29)

for some unique pair of
(i,j)
. This captures that each
\vec{X}_{i}
can only be correlated with two Brownian motions
dW_{i}
and
dW_{j}
for some
j\neq i
. Note, however, the drift
\mu_{i}
and diffusion
\vec{\sigma}_{i,\cdot}
can be functions of the entire process
\vec{X}
.

The general Milstein scheme is

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i}(t+h)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}_{i}(t)+\mu_{i}(\widetilde{X}(t))h+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sigma_{ik}(\widetilde{X}(t))\Delta W_{k}+\sum_{k,j=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ik}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{j}\Delta W_{k}+A_{(j,k)}),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ik}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}},k=1,\dots,d.

We can use the form of (
E.29
) to reduce this to

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i}(t+h)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}_{i}(t)+\mu_{i}(\widetilde{X}(t))h+\sigma_{ii}(\widetilde{X}(t))\Delta W_{i}+\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))\Delta W_{j}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}^{i}\sigma_{ii}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{i}\Delta W_{i}+A_{(i,i)})+\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ii}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{j}\Delta W_{i}+A_{(j,i)})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}^{i}\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{i}\Delta W_{j}+A_{(i,j)})+\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{j}\Delta W_{j}+A_{(j,j)}),

giving that

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{i}(t+h)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{X}_{i}(t)+\mu_{i}(\widetilde{X}(t))h+\sigma_{ii}(\widetilde{X}(t))\Delta W_{i}+\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))\Delta W_{j}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}^{i}\sigma_{ii}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{i}\Delta W_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))(\Delta W_{j}\Delta W_{j})

\displaystyle+[\mathcal{L}^{i}\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t)-\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ij}(\widetilde{X}(t))][\Delta W_{i}\Delta W_{j}+A_{(i,j)}].

Our hypothesis implies that
\mu_{i}
and
\sigma_{ik}
are at most linear functions, and so

\displaystyle\mu_{i}(\vec{X}(t))\leq\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert{X}_{k}(t)\rvert

\displaystyle\sigma_{ik}(\vec{X}(t))\leq\beta\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert{X}_{k}(t)\rvert

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{j}\sigma_{ik}(\vec{X}(t))\leq\beta^{2}d\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert{X}_{k}(t)\rvert

for constants
\alpha
and
\beta
. Hence we can write

\displaystyle\lvert\widetilde{X}_{i}(T-1)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\widetilde{X}_{i}(T-2)\rvert+\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\widetilde{X}_{k}(T-2)\rvert h+\beta\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\widetilde{X}_{k}(T-2)\rvert[\lvert\Delta W_{i}(T-1)\rvert+\lvert\Delta W_{j}(T-1)\rvert]

\displaystyle+\beta^{2}d\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\widetilde{X}_{k}(T-2)\rvert[(\Delta W_{i}(T-1))^{2}+(\Delta W_{j}(T-1))^{2}]+2\beta^{2}d\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\widetilde{X}_{k}(T-2)\rvert[\lvert\Delta W_{i}(T-1)\Delta W_{j}(T-1)\rvert

\displaystyle+\lvert A_{(i,j)}(T-1)\rvert]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert\widetilde{X}_{k}(T-2)\rvert\bigg(\alpha h+\beta\lvert\Delta W_{i}(T-1)\rvert+\beta\lvert\Delta W_{j}(T-1)\rvert

\displaystyle+\beta^{2}d(\Delta W_{i}(T-1))^{2}+\beta d(\Delta W_{j}(T-1))^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert\Delta W_{i}(T-1)\Delta W_{j}(T-1)\rvert+\beta^{2}d\lvert A_{(i,j)}(T-1)\rvert\bigg),

where
\widetilde{X}(T-2)
is independent of
\Delta W_{i}(T-1),\Delta W_{j}(T-1),A_{(i,j)}(T-1)
.

The integration problem is

\displaystyle\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{\lceil d/2\rceil})^{\times T}}f\circ g(\vec{z},\vec{a})d\vec{z}d\vec{a}.

We need to bound the integral outside of the hypercube
\mathcal{M}=[-R,R]^{T(d+\lceil d/2\rceil)}
in terms of
R
. By our assumptions on
f
, we can focus on bounding

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert g_{i,T-1}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert d\vec{z}d\vec{a},

where
g_{i,T-1}
constructs
\widetilde{X}_{i}(T-1)
for
i
. To bound this, we need to unroll the recursion in
g_{i,T-1}
. For
k\leq d
, we will use
k^{\prime}
to denote the unqiue index
\neq k
such that
b_{k,k^{\prime}}\neq 0
. This leads to

\displaystyle\lvert g_{i,T-1}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert g_{k,T-2}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert\bigg(\alpha h+\beta\lvert w_{i,T-1}\rvert+\beta\lvert w_{j,T-1}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert w_{i,T-1}w_{j,T-1}\rvert

\displaystyle+\beta^{2}d\lvert a_{i,j,T-1}\rvert\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\alpha h+\beta\lvert w_{i,T-1}\rvert+\beta\lvert w_{j,T-1}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert w_{i,T-1}w_{j,T-1}\rvert+\beta^{2}d\lvert a_{i,j,T-1}\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left[\left(\alpha h+\beta\lvert w_{k,T-1}\rvert+\beta\lvert w_{k^{\prime},T-2}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{k,T-2})^{2}+\beta^{2}d(w_{k,T-2})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert w_{k,T-2}w_{k^{\prime},T-2}\rvert+\beta^{2}d\lvert a_{k,k^{\prime},T-2}\rvert\right)\right]

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{d}\lvert g_{k,T-3}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert.

Observing the pattern, we see that

\displaystyle\lvert g_{i,T-1}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}\left(\alpha h+\beta\lvert w_{i,T-1}\rvert+\beta\lvert w_{j,T-1}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d(w_{i,T-1})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert a_{i,j,T-1}\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{t=0}^{T-2}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}\alpha h+\beta\lvert w_{k,t}\rvert+\beta\lvert w_{k^{\prime},t}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{k,t})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert w_{k,t}w_{k^{\prime},t}\rvert+\beta^{2}d(w_{k,t})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert a_{k,k^{\prime},t}\rvert\right),

so

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert g_{i,T-1}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d}\alpha h+\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w_{k,t}\rvert+\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w_{k^{\prime},t}\rvert+\beta^{2}hd(w_{k,t})^{2}+\beta^{2}hd(w_{k,t})^{2}+\beta^{2}d\lvert w_{k,t}w_{k^{\prime},t}\rvert+\beta^{2}dh\lvert a_{k,k^{\prime},t}\rvert\right),

where we have implicitly rescaled the
w
and
a
, pulling out the step-size
h
. Then, integrating and using the i.i.d. property over time

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lvert g_{i,T-1}(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}d^{T}\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\alpha h+\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w_{1}\rvert+\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w_{2}\rvert+\beta hd(w_{1})^{2}+\beta hd(w_{2})^{2}+\beta d\lvert w_{1}w_{2}\rvert+\beta dh\lvert a_{1,2}\rvert\right)^{T}
