\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}d^{T}\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\left[\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w\rvert+2\beta hd(w)^{2}]+\beta dh\lvert a_{1,2}\rvert\right]+\beta dh\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\lvert w\rvert\right)^{2}\right)^{T}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}Td^{T}\left(\int\left[\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w\rvert+2\beta hd(w)^{2}]+\beta^{2}dh\lvert a_{1,2}\rvert\right]+\beta^{2}dh\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\lvert w\rvert\right)^{2}\right)^{T-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\int\left[\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w\rvert+2\beta^{2}hd(w)^{2}]+\beta^{2}dh\lvert a_{1,2}\rvert\right]+\beta^{2}dh\left(\int\lvert w\rvert\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\widetilde{X}(0)\rVert_{1}Td^{T}\left(\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}+2\beta^{2}hd+\beta^{2}dhC+\beta dh\right)^{T-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left[\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\left[\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}\lvert w\rvert+2\beta^{2}hd(w)^{2}]+\beta^{2}dh\lvert a_{1,2}\rvert\right]+\beta^{2}dh\left(\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\lvert w\rvert\right)^{2}\right]

where the second-to-last inequality follows from the union bound and
C
is the universal constant
C=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert a_{(1,2)}\rvert da_{(1,2)}
.
Hence the problem has be reduced to analyzing the truncation error of a triple integral.

Hence there are only two kinds of integrals we need to worry about. One is the simple Gaussian

\displaystyle\int_{\lvert w\rvert\geq c}\lvert w\rvert^{2}p_{\mathcal{N}}(w)dw,

where it suffices to take
c=\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)
to make
\epsilon

The second one is for the Lévy . We know that the marginal moment generating function of the Levy is
[
Lév51
,
GL94
]
:
\mathbb{E}[e^{xA}]=\frac{1}{\cos(x)}
. Thus by Chernoff,
\mathbb{P}[\lvert a\rvert\geq c]\leq 2e^{-2\pi c}

Following, effectively, the same arguments in the proof of Lemma
F
, one can obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{\lvert a\rvert\geq c}^{\infty}\lvert a\rvert p(a)da

\displaystyle=c\mathbb{P}[\lvert a\rvert\geq c]+\int_{c}^{\infty}x\mathbb{P}[\lvert a\rvert\geq x]dx

\displaystyle\leq c\mathbb{P}[\lvert a\rvert\geq c]+\int_{c}^{\infty}2xe^{-2\pi x}dx

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\left(ce^{-2\pi c}\right),

so
c=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\epsilon)\right)
suffices.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}^{c}}\lvert f\circ g(\vec{z},\vec{a})\rvert d\vec{z}d\vec{a}\leq B\lVert\widehat{X}(0)\rVert_{1}T^{2}d^{T}\left(\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}+2\beta hd+\beta dhC+\beta dh\right)^{T-1}\left[\left(\alpha h+2\beta\sqrt{h}+4\beta hd\right)\epsilon\right],

so we can truncate the
\vec{w}
and
\vec{a}
to
\ell_{\infty}
balls of size
\mathcal{O}\left(T\log(\lVert B\widehat{X}(0)\rVert_1Td/\epsilon)\right)
for an overall error of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
.

∎

Appendix F
Additional Technical Lemmas

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
h:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},p:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be two continuous functions over compact set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}
, and
\epsilon_{\textup{poly}}:=\lVert h-\sqrt{p}\rVert_{\infty}
. For
\epsilon_{\text{poly}}<1
, we have

\lVert p-h^{2}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 3\epsilon_{\textup{poly}},

.

Proof.

For
\epsilon_{\text{poly}}<1
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert p-h^{2}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\sqrt{p}-h\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\sqrt{p}+h\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\sqrt{p}-h\rVert_{\infty}\left[2\lVert\sqrt{p}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\sqrt{p}-h\rVert_{\infty}\right]\leq 3\epsilon_{\text{poly}}.

∎

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\arg(z)\in[-\frac{\pi}{4},\frac{\pi}{4}]\cup[\frac{3\pi}{4},\frac{5\pi}{4}]
}
, then the functions
\frac{z}{\sinh(z)}
and
\frac{z}{\tanh(z)}
can be uniformly approximate to
\epsilon
additive error on
\mathcal{D}(r)\cap\mathcal{R}
by an
\mathcal{O}(\log(r/\epsilon))
degree polynomial, where
\mathcal{D}(r)
is the closed disc of radius
r
around zero in
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

It is known that we have the following series expansions for
\frac{z}{\sinh(z)}
and
\frac{z}{\tanh(z)}
:

\displaystyle\frac{z}{\sinh(z)}=(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))}2ze^{(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))+1}z}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))+1}2zk}

\displaystyle\frac{z}{\tanh(z)}=(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))}z(1+e^{(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))+1}2z})\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}e^{(-1)^{\text{sgn}(\text{Re}(z))+1}2zk}

\displaystyle\frac{z}{\sinh(z)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2(1-2^{2n-1})B_{2n}z^{2n}}{(2n)!},\lvert z\rvert\in(0,\pi)

\displaystyle\frac{z}{\tanh(z)}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{2^{2n}B_{2n}z^{2n}}{(2n)!},\lvert z\rvert\in(0,\pi),

where
B_{2n}
are the Bernoulli numbers.

Let
\epsilon
denote the desired polynomial approximation error.
For the first two series expansions for
z\csch(z)
and
z\coth(z)
we have that the truncation error when keeping
k-1
terms is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{2\lvert z\rvert e^{-\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}}{\lvert 1-e^{-2\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}\rvert}e^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}=\frac{\lvert z\rvert e^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}}{\lvert\sinh(\text{Re}(z))\rvert}\leq e^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert},

\displaystyle\frac{\lvert z(1+e^{-2\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert})\rvert}{\lvert 1-e^{-2\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}\rvert}e^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}=\frac{ze^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}}{\lvert\tanh(\text{Re}(z))\rvert}\leq ze^{-2k\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert},

respectively. Hence since
z\in\mathcal{D}(r)
if suffices to take
k=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert}\log(r/\epsilon)\right)
for an
\epsilon
truncation error. If
\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert\geq 1
, then we can just take
k=\mathcal{O}(\log(r/\epsilon))
.

For the other case, since
z\in\mathcal{R}
,
\lvert\text{Re}(z)\rvert<1\implies\lvert z\rvert<\sqrt{2}<\pi
. Thus, we are within the radius of convergence of the second pair of series. Also,
k=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\epsilon)\right)
suffices for those series.
∎

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Consider an integral
\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\vec{x})d\vec{x}
, where
\mathcal{X}=[a_{L}^{(1)},a_{U}^{(1)}]\times\cdots\times[a_{L}^{(d)},a_{U}^{(d)}]
is a rectangular region in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\Delta_{k}=\frac{a_{U}^{(k)}-a_{L}^{(k)}}{N}
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{N}(f)
is the left-endpoint Riemann sum which takes
N
uniform grid points in each dimension, and let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathcal{X}
define the grid. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}
denote the cell associated with grid point
\vec{x}
in
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}=\vec{x}+\prod_{k}[0,\Delta_{k}]
. Then

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{R}_{N}(f)-\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\vec{x})d\vec{x}\rvert\leq\left[\sum_{\vec{x}\in\mathcal{M}}\sup_{\vec{y}\in C_{\vec{x}}}\lVert\nabla f(\vec{y})\rVert_{\infty}\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Delta_{k}\right]\frac{\textup{diam}(\mathcal{X})}{N}.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}=[x_{1},x_{1}+\Delta_{1}]\times\cdots\times[x_{d},x_{d}+\Delta_{d}]
. Then,

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{R}_{N}(f)-\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\vec{x})d\vec{x}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\vec{x}\in\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}}\lvert f(\vec{x})-f(\vec{y})\rvert d\vec{y}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\vec{x}\in\mathcal{M}}\sup_{\vec{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}}\lVert\nabla f(\vec{y})\rVert_{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}}\lVert\vec{x}-\vec{y}\rVert_{1}d\vec{y}.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}}\lVert\vec{x}-\vec{y}\rVert_{1}d\vec{y}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\vec{x}}}\lvert x_{k}-y_{k}\rvert d\vec{y}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Delta_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\Delta_{k}}{2}\leq\frac{\textup{diam}(\mathcal{X})}{N}\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Delta_{k}.

So the result follows.
∎

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
{X}
be a random variable with pdf
p(x)
. Suppose
\beta>0
, and that
e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]
is integrable on
[0,\infty)
. Then for any
\alpha\in[0,\infty)

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}e^{\beta x}p(x)dx=e^{\beta\alpha}\mathbb{P}[X\geq\alpha]+\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\beta e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]dy.

Proof.

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}e^{\beta x}p(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbb{1}_{x\geq\alpha}p(x)\int_{-\infty}^{x}\beta e^{\beta y}dydx

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\beta e^{\beta y}\int_{y}^{\infty}\mathbb{1}_{x\geq\alpha}p(x)dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\beta e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq\max(\alpha,y)]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\alpha}\beta e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq\alpha]dy+\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\beta e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]dy

\displaystyle=e^{\beta\alpha}\mathbb{P}[X\geq\alpha]+\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\beta e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]dy

∎

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
{X}
be a random variable with pdf
p(x)
. Suppose
\beta>0
, and that
e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\theta-\beta)y}\right)
with
\theta>\beta
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}e^{\beta x}p(x)dx=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\theta-\beta)\alpha}\right).

Proof.

If we have
e^{\beta y}\mathbb{P}[X\geq y]=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\theta-\beta)y}\right)
for
\theta>\beta
. Then the above is upper bounded by

\displaystyle e^{-(\theta-\beta)\alpha}+\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\beta e^{-(\theta-\beta)y}dy=(1+\frac{\beta}{\theta-\beta})e^{-(\theta-\beta)\alpha}=\mathcal{O}\left(e^{-(\theta-\beta)\alpha}\right).

∎

Lemma
\theTheorem
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be
\chi^{2}_{r}
distributed, then for
m<\frac{1}{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\exp(my)p(y)dy=(1-2m)^{-r/2}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{Y}\geq(1-2m)\alpha].

Proof.

We will apply a change of variables
y=\frac{z}{1-2m}
.

\displaystyle\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\exp(my)p(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\alpha}^{\infty}\frac{y^{r/2-1}e^{-y(1/2-m)}}{2^{r/2}\Gamma(r/2)}dy

\displaystyle=(1-2m)^{-r/2}\int_{(1-2m)\alpha}^{\infty}\frac{z^{r/2-1}e^{-z/2}}{2^{r/2}\Gamma(r/2)}dz

\displaystyle=(1-2m)^{-r/2}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{Y}\geq(1-2m)\alpha].

∎

Appendix G
Review of Fixed-Point Coherent Arithmetic

We consider fixed-point quantum arithmetic, i.e. approximating real numbers by
n
bits:

\displaystyle x:=\underbrace{x_{n-1}\cdots x_{n-p}}_{p}.\underbrace{x_{n-p-1}\cdot x_{0}}_{n-p}.

The idea is to implement reversible versions of arithmetic operations:

\displaystyle|x\rangle|0\rangle\rightarrow|x\rangle|f(x)\rangle,

for arithmetic function
f
.

G.1
Arithmetic for Square-root

The paper proposes implementing the inverse-square root function via Newton’s method,i.e.

\displaystyle x_{n+1}=x_{n}(1.5-\frac{ax_{n}^{2}}{2}),

so that
x_{n}\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\rightarrow}\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}
. They mention a sufficient initial guess is
2^{\lfloor-\frac{\lfloor\log_{2}a\rfloor}{2}\rfloor}
.
Note within a sufficient region of convergence Netwton’s method has quadratic convergence. The number of Toffolis required to implement
m
Newton steps is

\displaystyle T_{\text{invsqrt}}=n^{2}\left(\frac{15}{2}m+3\right)+15npm+n\left(\frac{23}{2}m+5\right)-15p^{2}m+15pm-2m.

The square-root function can be calculated by
x\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}
. The Toffoli count of multiplying two
n
-bit numbers is

\displaystyle T_{\text{mul}}(n,p)=\frac{3}{2}n^{2}+3np+\frac{3}{2}n-3p^{2}+3p,

where
n=\mathcal{O}(p)
gives

\displaystyle T_{\text{mul}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\right),

(G.1)

and

\displaystyle T_{\text{add}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n\right)

Note that the number of qubits used by the reversible implementation of Newton’s method grows linearly with the number of iterations. However, since Newton’s method (with good initialization) has
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\log(1/\epsilon)\right)
convergence.

Assuming we are in a region of quadratic convergence with
\epsilon<2^{-p}
. If
n=\mathcal{O}(p)
, we have that

\displaystyle T_{\text{sqrt}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\log\log(1/\epsilon)\right).

(G.2)

G.2
Arithmetic for Polynomials

According to
[
HRS18
]
we can implement a
d
degree polynomial in
x

\displaystyle P(x)=\sum_{k=0}a_{k}x^{k},

using

\displaystyle T_{\text{poly}}(n,d,p)=\frac{3}{2}n^{2}d+3npd+\frac{7}{2}nd-3p^{2}d+3pd-d

Toffoli gates. Taking
n=\mathcal{O}(p)
, we have in general

\displaystyle T_{\text{poly}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}d\right)

(G.3)

gates.

For
\sin^{-1}
we have for
\lvert x\rvert\leq 1
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d}\frac{(2n)!}{2^{2n}(n!)^{2}}\frac{x^{2n+1}}{2n+1},

and again we can take
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\epsilon)\right)
for
\epsilon
additive error over
[-1,1]
.

Note that if we are working with
n+p
bits, then we just need
\epsilon<2^{-p}
. Hence

\displaystyle T_{\sin^{-1}}=T_{\text{poly}}(n,p,p)=\frac{3}{2}n^{2}p+3np^{2}+\frac{7}{2}np-3p^{3}+3p^{2}-p,

assuming
n=\mathcal{O}(p)
. We have

\displaystyle T_{\sin^{-1}}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\log(1/\epsilon)\right).

(G.4)

Due to potential numerical instabilities near
x=\pm 1
,
[
HRS18
]
suggest changing coordinates for larger values of
x
and implementing
\sin^{-1}
via

\displaystyle\sin^{-1}\left(\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\right).
